8: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
8: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
8: Letter of Transmittal
8: Board of Education
8: January 15, 2026
8: January 15, 2026
8: Members of the Board of Managers and Citizens of the Houston Independent School District:
8: The Annual Comprehensive Financial Report (ACFR) of the Houston Independent School District (the “District”
8: or “HISD”) for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025 is hereby submitted. This document is management’s report of
8: financial operations to the Board of Managers (the “Board”), taxpayers, grantor agencies, employees, the Texas
8: Education Agency, and other interested parties.
8: The Texas Education Code requires that all school districts file an annual report with the Texas Education Agency
8: (TEA) within 150 days of the close of each fiscal year. However, due to delays in both the development of TEA’s
8: new audit application and the release of the final 2025 Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Compliance
8: Supplement, the submission deadline has been extended to February 2026. The annual report must be prepared in
8: accordance with generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) as applicable to governmental entities and must
8: be audited by a firm of independent licensed certified public accountants. This Annual Comprehensive Financial
8: Report is submitted in fulfillment of that requirement. An audit was conducted in accordance with Government
8: Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller General of the United States and the requirements ofTitle 2 U.S. Code
8: of Federal Regulations Part 200, Uniform Administrative Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements
8: for FederalAwards Uniform Guidance and Texas Grant Management Standards (TxGMS). The financial statements
8: of the District have been audited by Weaver and Tidwell, L.L.P.
8: To the best of our knowledge and belief, the enclosed information is accurate in all material respects and is
8: reported in a manner designed to fairly present the financial position as well as the financial condition of the District.
8: Disclosures necessary to enable the reader to gain understanding of such financial operations have been included.
8: Responsibility for the accuracy and completeness of the data presented, as well as the presentation of this report,
8: rests with District management.
8: GAAP requires that management provide a narrative introduction, overview, and analysis to accompany the basic
8: financial statements in the form of Management’s Discussion and Analysis (MD&A). This letter of transmittal is
8: designed to complement the MD&A and should be read in conjunction with it. The District’s MD&A can be found
8: immediately following the report of the independent auditors.
8: PROFILE OF THE HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
8: The District was created and incorporated by an act of the Thirty-Eighth Legislature of the State of Texas in 1923.
8: As required by House Bill 1842, passed in 2015 by the Texas legislature, Texas Education Commissioner Mike
8: Morath appointed the nine-member Board of Managers to temporarily replace the elected board of trustees on
8: June 1, 2023. The Board of Managers has the same authority as the elected trustees and will be responsible for
8: overseeing the management of the school district. Based on legislative authority codified in the Texas Education
8: Code, the Board (1) has exclusive power and duty to govern and oversee the District; (2) can acquire and hold real
8: and personal property, sue and be sued, and hold all rights and titles to school property; (3) shall have power to levy
8: and collect taxes and to issue bonds; (4) can contract for appointed officers, teachers, and other personnel as well
8: as for goods and services; and (5) has the right of eminent domain to acquire real property.
8: HISD, with an enrollment of 176,727 students, is the tenth-largest public school system in the nation and the largest
8: in Texas. The District encompasses 333 square miles within Harris County, Texas including 51% of the current
8: geographic area of the City of Houston and all or part of four other cities or villages. HISD is an ethnically diverse
8: district as 61.8 percent of all students are Hispanic, 20.9 percent are African American, 9.9 percent are White, 5.1
8: percent are Asian and 2.3 percent are of other ethnicities.
9: - 3 -
9: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
9: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
9: Houston Independent School District provides services through 274 schools, of which 8 are early childhood centers
9: (pre-kindergarten for four-year-old students), 159 elementary schools (grades K-5), 39 middle schools (grades 6-8),
9: 37 high schools (grades 9-12) and 31 combination/alternative sites. The average age of the District’s schools is 46
9: years. A list of school buildings with year of construction can be found in the Statistical Data Section. (See pages
9: 131-152)
9: Four division superintendents oversee the elementary, middle, high school and alternative/charter schools.
9: Approximately four senior executive directors, twelve executive directors of instruction, and four executive directors
9: of support report to the division superintendents. These individuals provide leadership to principals, align resources
9: and support for teachers, and ensure that the district is providing equitable and quality educational opportunities to
9: students. HISD’s organization is designed to emphasize teaching and learning, align school goals and programs
9: for sustained improvement, eliminate duplicated services, and provide greater oversight of data and compliance
9: with state laws and regulations.
9: The District serves its diverse student population through an intensive core curriculum and specialized instructional
9: andcareerprograms.TheDistrictprovidesafullrangeofprogramsandservicestoitsstudents,includingelementary
9: and secondary courses for general, vocational, and college preparatory levels, as well as magnet, and gifted and
9: talented programs.Academic programs are supplemented by a variety of co-curricular and extracurricular activities.
9: In addition to the regular educational programs, the District offers programs in special education, bilingual, and
9: compensatory education. HISD contracted for the operation of 7 external performance (charter) schools which are
9: funded with state funds that flow through the District.
9: ECONOMIC OUTLOOK
9: Houston is the largest city in Texas and the fourth most populous city in the United States with a population of 2.4
9: million, and 7.8 million people in the Houston metro area. The region’s largest industries are energy, aerospace and
9: aviation, life sciences and biotechnology, and transportation and logistics. Employment in the Houston Metropolitan
9: StatisticalArea stood at 3.730 million people in June 2025 per the Texas Workforce Commission, up 1% percent from
9: one year earlier. Houston’s economy continues to grow, but at a slower pace than in previous years. Per the Greater
9: Houston Partnership, Houston’s economy is performing well but uncertainty and rising material costs are leading
9: businesses to take a cautious posture. Local sales and use tax collections are up, indicating robust spending.
9: Construction and airport traffic are down when compared to last year, but home and vehicle sales have risen.
9: LONG-TERM FINANCIAL PLANNING
9: The District is subject to the state’s target revenue formula for school district funding. Under the allocation
9: methodology, the District’s maintenance and operations revenue from property taxes and state aid is determined
9: by a state formula based on weighted average daily attendance. As collections from property taxes increase, state
9: aid is reduced. The District is challenged with providing salary increases and other inflationary fixed costs within
9: this allocation methodology. The District is constantly adjusting future projections as costs in the market shift and as
9: changes are proposed or adopted by the Texas Legislature.
9: Enrollment decreased from the 2023-24 school year by 7,382 students. The 2025-26 budget includes an additional
9: reduction of 6,528 students. As of October 2025, the District’s certified net taxable value for the 2025 tax year
9: increased by 2.8 percent. The collection rate for fiscal year 2025 was 97 percent. The District expects to maintain
9: a 97 percent collection rate.
9: With the 2017 fiscal year, the Houston Independent School District began paying recapture, now known as
9: “contracted instructional services between public schools”. Under the current state finance system, the District
9: sends funds to the state if local revenue exceeds its entitlement. As of June 30, 2025, the District’s “contracted
9: instructional services between public schools” expense was $56.9 million.
9: Board of Education
9: January 15, 2026
10: - 4 -
10: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
10: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
10: ACCOUNTING AND BUDGETARY CONTROLS
10: The diverse nature of the District’s operations and the necessity of legal compliance preclude recording the financial
10: transactions of the District in a single fund. The District’s accounting system is organized and operated on a “fund
10: basis.” Each fund is a distinct, self-balancing accounting entity. The various funds are fully described in Note 1 of
10: the “Notes to the Basic Financial Statements” starting on page 39.
10: In developing, evaluating, and improving the District’s accounting system, consideration is given to the adequacy
10: of the internal control structure. Internal controls are designed to provide management with reasonable, but not
10: absolute, assurance that assets are safeguarded against loss from unauthorized use or disposition and those
10: transactions are executed in accordance with management’s authorization and recorded properly to permit the
10: preparation of financial statements in accordance with GAAP. The concept of reasonable assurance recognizes
10: that the cost of a control should not exceed the benefits likely to be derived, and the evaluation of costs and benefits
10: requires estimates and judgments by management.
10: All internal control evaluations occur within the above-mentioned framework. We believe the District’s internal
10: control structure adequately safeguards assets and provides reasonable assurance of the proper recording of
10: financial transactions.
10: As a recipient of federal, state, and local grants, the District is also responsible for an adequate internal control
10: structure that ensures compliance with applicable laws and regulations related to these grants.All funded grants are
10: subject to testing as part of the District’s Single Audit as mandated by the Uniform Guidance. The internal control
10: structure is subject to ongoing reviews by management. As a part of the District’s Single Audit, tests are conducted
10: to determine the adequacy of the internal control structure as related to state and federal award programs as well
10: as compliance with applicable laws and regulations. The SingleAudit reports are included in the compliance section
10: of this report.
10: The District utilizes a line-item budget of proposed expenditures and the means of financing them. The emphasis
10: of the budget process is to identify the activities requiring resources and to rank those activities according to the
10: needs of the entire District. Budgetary control is maintained at the function level by organizational unit through the
10: encumbrance of estimated purchase amounts to coincide with the execution of contracts, approval of personnel
10: transactions, or release of purchase orders to vendors. The Board approves annual budgets for the General Fund,
10: Debt Service Fund, and Nutrition Services Fund. Budgets for Special Revenue funds (other than the Food Service
10: Fund) and Capital Projects Fund are prepared on a project basis, based on grant regulations or applicable bond
10: requirements.
10: MAJOR INITIATIVES
10: Houston ISD has continued advancing several major academic, operational, and governance initiatives aligned with
10: Superintendent Miles’ Destination 2035 framework and the New Education System (NES).
10: Academic and Instructional Initiatives
10: • Curriculum Standardization: Nearly all HISD campuses—all but five—have opted into the district-developed
10: curriculum for implementation in the 2025-26 school year, ensuring consistent, high-quality instructional
10: materials across schools.
10: • Expansion of NES Campuses: The district extended the NES model to 45 additional campuses in 2024-25,
10: reallocating central resources to support historically underperforming schools. It is important to note that of
10: the 121 D and F school the current administration started with there are only 18 D schools left at the end of
10: school year 2025. Also, the goal is by 2027 there will only be A and B schools.
10: Human Capital and Teacher Quality
10: • HISD issued over 7,000 teacher contracts for the 2025-26 school year, with increased emphasis on
10: certification compliance and performance accountability.
10: Board of Education
10: January 15, 2026
11: - 5 -
11: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
11: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
11: Board of Education
11: January 15, 2026
11: • Teacher staffing adjustments reflect continued focus on instructional excellence and differentiated
11: compensation models introduced under NES.
11: Operational Improvements
11: • Transportation System Overhaul: HISD restructured the transportation department for 2025-26, redesigning
11: roles and setting new ride-time standards to enhance efficiency and student access.
11: • Unified Family Communication Platform: The district launched ParentSquare, consolidating multiple
11: systems into a single communication platform for parents and guardians.
11: • Facilities and Infrastructure Upgrades: Following the defeat of the 2024 bond proposal, HISD approved
11: approximately $120 million in Maintenance Tax Notes to address priority facility needs, including HVAC,
11: roofing, technology, and transportation upgrades, and to fund development of the new Career and Technical
11: Education (CTE) Center at the Grimes Facility.
11: Governance and Oversight
11: • The Texas Education Agency (TEA) extended its intervention in HISD through June 2027, with four new
11: members appointed to the Board of Managers to continue oversight of district reforms and performance
11: outcomes.
11: Collectively, these initiatives reflect HISD’s continued progress toward sustainable academic improvement,
11: operational efficiency, and accountability. The district remains focused on aligning resources, systems, and
11: governance with long-term strategic goals under Destination 2035.
11: AWARDS AND ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
11: Perhaps no responsibility is as great as that of educating our youth, and all of our efforts lead back to the classroom.
11: Given that the District’s academic advancements are dependent upon its fiscal successes, it is imperative that HISD
11: continue to improve upon its reporting proficiencies. The following text cites awards received by the District for
11: financial reporting excellence and the related acknowledgments in this regard.
11: ASBO Certificate of Excellence
11: The District received the Association of School Business Officials’ (“ASBO”) Certificate of Excellence in Financial
11: Reporting for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024. The District has received this award for 46 consecutive years.
11: This award certifies that the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report substantially conforms to the principles and
11: standards of financial reporting as recommended and adopted by ASBO.
11: GFOA Certificate of Achievement
11: The Government Finance Officers Association of the United States and Canada (“GFOA”) awarded a Certificate
11: of Achievement for Excellence in Financial Reporting to the District for its Annual Comprehensive Financial Report
11: for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024. This was the 52st consecutive year that the District has achieved this
11: prestigious award.
11: GTOT Certificate of Distinction
11: The District received the Government Treasurers’ Organization of Texas (“GTOT”) Certificate of Distinction in
11: October 2024. The certificate is good for a two-year period. This award recognized the District’s commitment to
11: maintaining a comprehensive written investment policy that meets the criteria set forth in the GTOT Investment
11: Policy Checklist.
11: TASBO Award of Merit for Purchasing Operations
11: The District received the Texas Association of School Business Officials’ (“TASBO”) Award of Merit of Purchasing
11: Operations in February 2025. This award honors school districts that uphold professional standards in acquiring
11: goods and services.

12: Moreover, we wish to thank and express appreciation to the administrators and other employees of the District

23: CPAs AND ADVISORS | WEAVER.COM
23: Independent Auditor’s Report
23: To the School Board and Management of
23: Houston Independent School District
23: Houston, Texas
23: Report on the Audit of the Financial Statements
23: Opinions
23: We have audited the financial statements of the governmental activities, the business-type activities, each major
23: fund, and the aggregate remaining fund information of Houston Independent School District (the District), as of
23: and for the year ended June 30, 2025, and the related notes to the financial statements, which collectively comprise
23: the District’s basic financial statements as listed in the table of contents.
23: In our opinion, the accompanying financial statements present fairly, in all material respects, the respective financial
23: position of the governmental activities, the business-type activities, each major fund, and the aggregate remaining
23: fund information of the District, as of June 30, 2025 and the respective changes in financial position and, where
23: applicable, cash flows thereof for the year then ended in accordance with accounting principles generally accepted
23: in the United States of America.
23: Basis for Opinions
23: We conducted our audit in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States of America
23: (GAAS) and the standards applicable to financial audits contained in Government Auditing Standards, issued by the
23: Comptroller General of the United States. Our responsibilities under those standards are further described in the
23: Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of the Financial Statements section of our report. We are required to be
23: independent of the District and to meet our other ethical responsibilities, in accordance with the relevant ethical
23: requirements relating to our audit. We believe that the audit evidence we have obtained is sufficient and appropriate
23: to provide a basis for our audit opinions.
23: Emphasis of Matter
23: As discussed in Note 1 to the basic financial statements, during the year ended June 30, 2025, the District
23: implemented Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) Statement No. 101, Compensated Absences.
23: Beginning net position has been restated as a result of the implementation of this statement. Our opinions are not
23: modified with respect to this matter.
23: Responsibilities of Management for the Financial Statements
23: The District’s management is responsible for the preparation and fair presentation of the financial statements in
23: accordance with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States of America, and for the design,
23: implementation, and maintenance of internal control relevant to the preparation and fair presentation of financial
23: statements that are free from material misstatement, whether due to fraud or error.
23: In preparing the financial statements, management is required to evaluate whether there are conditions or events,
23: considered in the aggregate, that raise substantial doubt about the District’s ability to continue as a going concern
23: for twelve months beyond the financial statement date, including any currently known information that may raise
23: substantial doubt shortly thereafter.
23: Independent Auditor’s Report
24: - 14 -
24: The School Board and Management of
24: Houston Independent School District
24: Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of the Financial Statements
24: Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements as a whole are free from
24: material misstatement, whether due to fraud or error, and to issue an auditor’s report that includes our opinions.
24: Reasonable assurance is a high level of assurance but is not absolute assurance and therefore is not a guarantee
24: that an audit conducted in accordance with GAAS and Government Auditing Standards will always detect a material
24: misstatement when it exists. The risk of not detecting a material misstatement resulting from fraud is higher than
24: for one resulting from error, as fraud may involve collusion, forgery, intentional omissions, misrepresentations, or
24: the override of internal control. Misstatements are considered material if there is a substantial likelihood that,
24: individually or in the aggregate, they would influence the judgment made by a reasonable user based on the
24: financial statements.
24: In performing an audit in accordance with GAAS and Government Auditing Standards, we:
24:  Exercise professional judgment and maintain professional skepticism throughout the audit.
24:  Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of the financial statements, whether due to fraud or
24: error, and design and perform audit procedures responsive to those risks. Such procedures include
24: examining, on a test basis, evidence regarding the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements.
24:  Obtain an understanding of internal control relevant to the audit in order to design audit procedures that
24: are appropriate in the circumstances, but not for the purpose of expressing an opinion on the effectiveness
24: of the District’s internal control. Accordingly, no such opinion is expressed.
24:  Evaluate the appropriateness of accounting policies used and the reasonableness of significant accounting
24: estimates made by management, as well as evaluate the overall presentation of the financial statements.
24:  Conclude whether, in our judgment, there are conditions or events, considered in the aggregate, that raise
24: substantial doubt about the District’s ability to continue as a going concern for a reasonable period of time.
24: We are required to communicate with those charged with governance regarding, among other matters, the planned
24: scope and timing of the audit, significant audit findings, and certain internal control–related matters that we
24: identified during the audit.
24: Required Supplementary Information
24: Accounting principles generally accepted in the United States of America require that the Management’s Discussion
24: and Analysis, Schedule of Revenues, Expenditures, and Changes in Fund Balance – Budget and Actual – General
24: Fund, Schedule of the District’s Proportionate Share of the Net Pension Liability – Teacher Retirement System,
24: Schedule of the District’s Contributions for Pension – Teacher Retirement System, Schedule of District’s
24: Proportionate Share of the Net OPEB Liability – Teacher Retirement System, and Schedule of the District’s
24: Contributions for Other Postemployment Benefits – Teacher Retirement System, be presented to supplement the
24: basic financial statements. Such information is the responsibility of management and, although not a part of the
24: basic financial statements, is required by the Governmental Accounting Standards Board who considers it to be an
24: essential part of financial reporting for placing the basic financial statements in an appropriate operational,
24: economic, or historical context. We have applied certain limited procedures to the required supplementary
24: information in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States of America, which
24: consisted of inquiries of management about the methods of preparing the information and comparing the
24: information for consistency with management’s responses to our inquiries, the basic financial statements, and other
24: knowledge we obtained during our audit of the basic financial statements. We do not express an opinion or provide
24: any assurance on the information because the limited procedures do not provide us with sufficient evidence to
24: express an opinion or provide any assurance.

25: Supplementary Information
25: Our audit was conducted for the purpose of forming opinions on the financial statements that collectively comprise
25: the District’s basic financial statements. The Combining and Individual Fund Statements and Schedules, Schedule of
25: Expenditures of Federal Awards as required by Title 2 U.S. Code of Federal Regulations, Part 200, Uniform
25: Administrative Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal Awards (Uniform Guidance), and
25: Schedule of Expenditures of State Awards, as required by Texas Grant Management Standards (TxGMS), are
25: presented for purposes of additional analysis and are not a required part of the basic financial statements.
25: The Combining and Individual Fund Statements and Schedules, Schedule of Expenditures of Federal Awards and
25: Schedule of Expenditures of State Awards are the responsibility of management and were derived from and relate
25: directly to the underlying accounting and other records used to prepare the basic financial statements. Such
25: information has been subjected to the auditing procedures applied in the audit of the basic financial statements
25: and certain additional procedures, including comparing and reconciling such information directly to the underlying
25: accounting and other records used to prepare the basic financial statements or to the basic financial statements
25: themselves, and other additional procedures in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United
25: States of America. In our opinion, the Combining and Individual Fund Statements and Schedules, Schedule of
25: Expenditures of Federal Awards and Schedule of Expenditures of State Awards are fairly stated, in all material
25: respects, in relation to the basic financial statements as a whole.
25: Other Information Included in the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report (ACFR)
25: Management is responsible for the other information included in the ACFR. The other information comprises the
25: Introductory Section and Statistical Section but does not include the financial statements and our auditor's report
25: thereon. Our opinions on the financial statements do not cover the other information, and we do not express an
25: opinion or any form of assurance thereon. In connection with our audit of the financial statements, our responsibility
25: is to read the other information and consider whether a material inconsistency exists between the other information
25: and the financial statements, or the other information otherwise appears to be materially misstated. If, based on the
25: work performed, we conclude that an uncorrected material misstatement of the other information exists, we are
25: required to describe it in our report.
25: Other Reporting Required by Government Auditing Standards
25: In accordance with Government Auditing Standards, we have also issued our report dated January 12, 2026 on our
25: consideration of the District’s internal control over financial reporting and on our tests of its compliance with certain
25: provisions of laws, regulations, contracts, and grant agreements and other matters. The purpose of that report is
25: solely to describe the scope of our testing of internal control over financial reporting and compliance and the results
25: of that testing, and not to provide an opinion on the effectiveness of internal control over financial reporting or on
25: compliance. That report is an integral part of an audit performed in accordance with GovernmentAuditing Standards
25: in considering the District’s internal control over financial reporting and compliance.

27: MANAGEMENT’S DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS
27: (UNAUDITED)
27: Our discussion and analysis of Houston Independent School District’s (the “District”) financial performance
27: provides an overview of the District’s financial activities for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025. It should be read
27: in conjunction with the transmittal letter at the front of this report and the basic financial statements which follow
27: this section.
27: The Management’s Discussion and Analysis provides information on both government-wide financial statements
27: and fund financial statements.
27: FINANCIAL HIGHLIGHTS
27: • The District’s total net position as presented on the Government-wide Statement of Net Position is $2.2
27: billion. For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025, the net position of the District decreased by $150 million.
27: • The District’s governmental funds financial statements reported a combined ending fund balance of
27: $1.031 billion. This balance consists of $748 million in the General Fund of which $33.71 million is
27: assigned, $127.38 million is restricted, $21.09 million is nonspendable, $101.13 million is committed, and
27: $464.76 million is unassigned and available for spending at the District’s discretion. The remaining
27: restricted fund balance of $299 million consists of the Special Revenue Fund, the Debt Service Fund,
27: and the Capital Project Fund. The Capital Project Fund’s fund balance also includes $(15.15) million in
27: unassigned.
27: • For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025, total revenue and transfers from governmental activities in the
27: Statement of Activities was $2.748 billion. Program revenues accounted for $575 million. General
27: revenues and transfers accounted for $2.175 billion.
27: • The General Fund had $1.940 billion in revenues of which $1.523 billion is property taxes. General Fund
27: expenditures and other financing sources were $2.25 billion and $11.47 million, respectively. This
27: resulted in a decrease to fund balance of $299.11 million.
27: • A portion of the debt obligations of the District are rated “Aaa” by Moody’s Investors Service, Inc.
27: (“Moody’s”) and “AAA” by Standard & Poor’s Global Ratings, a business unit of Standard & Poor’s
27: Financial Services LLC (“S&P”) by virtue of a guarantee by the Texas Permanent School Fund Bond
27: Guarantee Program. The outstanding unenhanced tax-supported debt of the District is rated “Aaa” by
27: Moody’s and “AA+” by S&P. In addition, lease revenue bonds issued by the District’s Public Facility
27: Corporation received ratings of “Aa1” by Moody’s. Rating information referenced is as of the last rating
27: reviews in February of 2025 by Moody’s and February of 2025 by S&P.
27: OVERVIEW OF THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
27: This discussion and analysis is intended to serve as an introduction to the District’s basic financial statements.
27: The District’s basic financial statements are comprised of three components: 1) government-wide financial
27: statements, 2) fund financial statements, and 3) notes to the basic financial statements. This report also contains
27: required supplementary information and other supplementary information in addition to the basic financial
27: statements themselves.
27: Government-Wide Financial Statements
27: The government-wide financial statements are designed to provide readers a broad overview of the District’s
27: finances in a manner similar to a private-sector business and are comprised of the Statement of Net Position and
27: the Statement of Activities. These statements provide information about the activities of the District as a whole
27: and present both long-term and short-term information about the District’s overall financial status. The District’s

27: OVERVIEW OF THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
27: This discussion and analysis is intended to serve as an introduction to the District’s basic financial statements.
27: The District’s basic financial statements are comprised of three components: 1) government-wide financial
27: statements, 2) fund financial statements, and 3) notes to the basic financial statements. This report also contains
27: required supplementary information and other supplementary information in addition to the basic financial
27: statements themselves.
27: Government-Wide Financial Statements
27: The government-wide financial statements are designed to provide readers a broad overview of the District’s
27: finances in a manner similar to a private-sector business and are comprised of the Statement of Net Position and
27: the Statement of Activities. These statements provide information about the activities of the District as a whole
27: and present both long-term and short-term information about the District’s overall financial status. The District’s
27: basic services are primarily financed by property taxes and include instruction, instructional resources and media
28: - 18 -
28: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
28: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
28: services, instructional staff development, instructional leadership, school leadership, guidance, counseling, and
28: evaluation services, social work services, health services, student transportation, food service, co-curricular/
28: extracurricular activities, general administration, plant maintenance and operations, security and monitoring
28: services, data processing services, community services, fiscal agent/member districts of shared services, juvenile
28: justice alternative education program, tax reinvestment zone payments, tax appraisal and collection, and interest
28: on long-term debt.
28: The Statement of Net Position presents information on all the District’s assets, deferred outflows, liabilities, and
28: deferred inflows with the difference reported as net position. Over time, increases or decreases in net position may
28: serve as a useful indicator of whether the District’s financial position is improving or deteriorating. The Statement
28: of Net Position includes the District’s non-fiduciary assets and liabilities.
28: The Statement of Activities presents information for all the current fiscal year’s revenues and expenses. This
28: activity is reported as soon as the underlying event giving rise to the activity occurs, regardless of the timing of
28: related cash flows. Therefore, revenues and expenses are reported in this statement for some items that will result
28: in cash flows in future fiscal periods (e.g., uncollected taxes and earned but unused vacation leave).
28: Both of the government-wide financial statements distinguish functions of the District that are principally supported
28: by taxes and intergovernmental revenues (governmental activities) from business-type activities that are intended
28: to recover all or a significant portion of their costs through user fees and charges.
28: Fund Financial Statements
28: The fund financial statements focus on individual parts of the government, reporting the District’s operations in
28: more detail than the government-wide statements. Funds are accounting devices that the District uses to keep
28: track of specific sources of funding and spending for particular purposes. Some funds are required by state law
28: and by bond covenants. The Board of Trustees establishes other funds to control and manage resources and
28: expenditures for particular purposes or to delineate the use of certain taxes and grants.
28: The District has three kinds of funds:
28: Governmental funds are used to account for essentially the same functions reported as governmental activities
28: in the government-wide financial statements. Most of the District’s activities are included in governmental funds
28: which focus on (1) how cash and other financial assets can readily be converted to cash flow in and out, and
28: (2) the balances left at year end that are available. However, unlike the government-wide financial statements,
28: governmental fund financial statements provide a detailed short-term view that helps determine whether there are
28: more or fewer financial resources that can be spent in the near future to finance the District’s programs. Because
28: this information does not encompass the additional long-term focus of the government-wide statements, we provide
28: additional information on the subsequent page that explains the relationship (or differences) between them.
28: The District maintains four governmental funds. Information is presented separately in the governmental fund
28: balance sheet and in the governmental fund statement of revenues, expenditures, and changes in fund balances
28: for the General Fund, the Special Revenue Fund, the Debt Service Fund, and the Capital Project Fund, all of which
28: are considered to be major funds.
28: Proprietary funds are used to account for operations that are financed similarly to those found in the private
28: sector. These funds provide both long and short-term financial information. The District maintains two types of
28: proprietary funds. Enterprise funds are used to report the same type of information as reported in the government-
28: wide financial statements. The District uses enterprise funds to account for the employee cafeteria (MarketPlace)
28: and Medicaid programs. The District uses internal service funds to report activities that provide services for the
28: District’s other programs and activities, i.e., health insurance, workers’compensation, print shop, and other internal
28: services. Because these services predominately benefit governmental rather than business-type functions, they
28: have been included within governmental activities within the government-wide financial statements.
28: Fiduciary funds are used to account for assets held by the District in a trustee capacity or as a custodian for
28: individuals, private organizations and/or other funds. The District uses fiduciary funds to account for scholarship
28: funds and student activity funds. No fiduciary funds are used as clearing accounts to distribute financial resources
29: - 19 -
29: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
29: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
29: funds and student activity funds. No fiduciary funds are used as clearing accounts to distribute financial resources
29: to other funds. The District is responsible for ensuring that the assets reported in these funds are used for their
29: intended purposes. All the District’s fiduciary activities are reported in a separate statement of fiduciary net
29: position and a statement of changes in fiduciary net position. We exclude these activities from the District’s
29: government-wide financial statements because the District cannot use these assets to finance its operations.
29: Notes to the Basic Financial Statements
29: The notes to the basic financial statements provide additional information that is essential to a complete
29: understanding of the data provided in the government-wide and fund financial statements.
29: Other Information
29: In addition to the basic financial statements and accompanying notes, this report also presents certain required
29: supplementary information that further explains and supports the information in the financial statements.
29: Immediately following the required supplementary information, combining statements are included for the
29: nonmajor enterprise funds and the internal service funds.

30: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
30: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
30: GOVERNMENT-WIDE FINANCIAL ANALYSIS
30: As noted earlier, net position may serve as a useful indicator of the District’s financial position. The District’s
30: combined net position decreased $144 million. The District’s net investment in capital assets is $2.45 billion and
30: includes investments in capital assets (e.g. land, buildings and improvements, furniture, equipment, right-to-use
30: assets, and construction in progress) less any related debt used to acquire those assets that is still outstanding.
30: The District uses these capital assets to provide services to students; consequently, these assets are not available
30: for future spending. Although the District’s investment in its capital assets is reported net of related debt, it should
30: be noted that the resources needed to repay this debt must be provided from other sources, since the capital
30: assets themselves cannot be used to liquidate these liabilities.
30: The District’s restricted net position of $241 million represents resources that are subject to external restrictions
30: on how they may be used. The remaining $(484.5) million is unrestricted net position.

30: The District's Condensed Statement of Net Position
30: The District implemented GASB Statement No. 101, Compensated Absences in fiscal year 2025 resulting in a
30: restatement which increased net position by $12,281,689 as of June 30, 2024. Because the restatement is the

30: combined net position decreased $144 million. The District’s net investment in capital assets is $2.45 billion and
30: includes investments in capital assets (e.g. land, buildings and improvements, furniture, equipment, right-to-use
30: assets, and construction in progress) less any related debt used to acquire those assets that is still outstanding.
30: The District uses these capital assets to provide services to students; consequently, these assets are not available
30: for future spending. Although the District’s investment in its capital assets is reported net of related debt, it should
30: be noted that the resources needed to repay this debt must be provided from other sources, since the capital
30: assets themselves cannot be used to liquidate these liabilities.
30: The District’s restricted net position of $241 million represents resources that are subject to external restrictions on
30: how they may be used. The remaining $(484.5) million is unrestricted net position.
30: The District implemented GASB Statement No. 101, Compensated Absences in fiscal year 2025 resulting in a
30: restatement which increased net position by $12,281,689 as of June 30, 2024. Because the restatement is the
30: result of a change in accounting principle, the information for 2024 in the tables in the MD&Ahave not been restated
30: in accordance with GASB Statement No. 100, Accounting Changes and Error Corrections – an amendment of
30: GASB Statement No. 62. See Note 1 for more information.

31: revenue is from property taxes. Operating grants and contributions provided 21 percent while another 6 percent
31: is provided by state aid, 2 percent from interest income, and 2 percent from other miscellaneous revenue.
31: Total revenues decreased by $385 million from the prior year due primarily to decreases in grants and
31: contributions.
31: Governmental Activities

31: operations due primarily to a decrease in operating grants and contributions. Funding for governmental activities
31: is by specific program revenue or through general revenues such as property taxes and investment earnings.

31: activities. These program revenues amounted to $573 million.
31: • The remaining cost of governmental activities not directly funded by program revenues was $2.317 billion
31: including $1.888 billion funded by property taxes and $151 million funded by state aid which is not
31: restricted to specific programs.

34: FINANCIAL ANALYSIS OF THE DISTRICT’S FUNDS
34: Governmental Funds For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025, the District’s governmental funds reported ending
34: fund balances of $1.032 billion. Of this amount, $448 million is unassigned and available for use in activities at the
34: District’s discretion. The remainder of the fund balance is designated as nonspendable, restricted, committed, or
34: assigned, which indicates that it is not available for new spending because it has already been obligated for grants,
34: debt service, bond projects and other purposes of the District.
34: The General Fund is the primary operating fund of the District. The fund balance of the General Fund decreased
34: $299.1 million during the fiscal year. At the end of the fiscal year, the unassigned fund balance of $464 million
34: represented 21 percent of the total General Fund expenditures.
34: The Debt Service Fund has a total fund balance of $156.4 million all of which is restricted for the payment of debt
34: service. The fund balance during the fiscal year increased by $32.6 million. The District makes semi-annual debt
34: service payments in December/June, January/July, February/August and March/September of each year. Debt
34: service payments for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025 included all scheduled payments including payments
34: due within 30 days of year end.
34: The Special Revenue Fund accounts for all grants received by the District from local, state, and federal sources.
34: The balance decreased by $12 million to $102 million due to a decrease in federal funds.
34: The Capital Project Fund has a total fund balance of $25.5 million. The restricted fund balance at the end of the
34: fiscal year totaled $40.69 million and represents bond funds for facility construction projects. The assigned fund
34: balance of $1.22 million represents encumbrances. The unassigned fund balance totaled $(16.37) million. The
34: fund balance decreased by $46.06 million due to expenditures for bond construction projects and transfers to the
34: general fund for debt service payments and capital projects.
34: ProprietaryFundsTheDistrictmaintainsbothenterprisefundsandinternalservicefunds. Informationispresented
34: separately in the proprietary funds statement of net position and in the proprietary funds statement of revenues,
34: expenses, and changes in net position. See pages 33 and 34..
34: CAPITAL ASSETS AND DEBT ADMINISTRATION
34: CAPITAL ASSETS
34: As of June 30, 2025, the District has invested $4.07 billion, net of depreciation and amortization, in a broad range
34: of capital assets, including land, buildings and improvements, furniture and equipment, right-to-use assets, and
34: vehicles. This represents a net decrease of $80 million from last year.
34: As of June 30, 2025, the District had fully funded construction commitments of $8.6 million, $7.7 million of which
34: has been spent to date and $90 million in remaining commitment. More detailed information about the District’s
34: capital assets is presented in Note 6 of the “Notes to the Basic Financial Statements.” See pages 57 – 59.
35: - 25 -
35: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
35: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
35: Long Term Debt (See Note 8)
35: As of June 30, 2025, the District had bonded debt outstanding of $1.559 billion. The ratio of net general bonded
35: debt to assessed valuation and the amount of bonded debt per student are useful indicators of the District’s
35: debt position. This data reflects a decrease in the ratio of net bonded debt to assessed value to 0.55 percent,
35: as compared to 0.74 percent last year.
35: The District debt totals included $0 in Variable Rate Bonds outstanding at fiscal year-end.
35: Outstanding variable-rate debt decreased by $190.435 million from the prior-year ending balance due
35: to redemptions funded through refunding bonds. In addition, on May 1, 2025, the District redeemed $92.26 million
35: in fixed-rate bonds as part of the same refunding transaction.
35: The District executed a targeted debt tender offer during the year ended June 30, 2025, which resulted in the
35: issuance of $20.55 million in refunding maintenance tax notes. The remaining outstanding balance of previously
35: issued maintenance tax notes was $143.210 million as of June 30, 2024.
35: On May 28, 2025, the District defeased $115.42 million in fixed-rate bonds utilizing available debt service funds.
35: The District did not issue any contractual obligations during the year ended June 30, 2025.
35: Interest earnings on proceeds from debt issued since May 15, 1989, are subject to arbitrage regulations contained
35: in the Federal Tax Reform Act of 1986. The District did not identify arbitrage liability as of June 30, 2025.
35: General Fund Budgetary Highlights
35: Over the course of the year, the District revised its budget several times. Significant budget adjustments include:
35: • Decrease in property tax revenues of $24.6 million due to the increase in exemptions and refunds
35: • Increase in foundation state revenues of $38.5 million
35: • Increase in expenditures dues to carryover encumbrances of $17.3 million
35: • Increase in school leadership of $6.7 million
35: • Increase in recapture of $44.5 million
35: • Increase of $47.7 million for facilities maintenance and transportation costs
35: • Increase in transfers from other funds in the amount of $7.7 million
35: Total actual expenditures were $74.7 million more than the final budget. Following were the key variances:
35: • $59.9 million over budget in instructional salary and Texas Connections expenditures
35: • $12.5 million in school leadership
35: • $12.4 million in recapture
35: The 2024-2025 final expenditure budget decreased by $51.0 million from the prior year budget.
35: The final budgeted amount for revenues was $1.906 billion. Actual revenues were greater than the budget by
35: $33.8 million primarily due to:
35: • $5.3 million under budget in property tax collections
35: • $1.5 million over budget in interest earnings
35: • $9.9 million over budget in the Foundation School Program
35: • $27.9 million over budget in other local sources
36: - 26 -
36: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
36: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
36: Budget amendments are presented to the Board of Education for consideration and approval throughout the year.
36: ECONOMIC FACTORS AND NEXT YEAR’S BUDGETS AND RATES
36: The District’s General Fund is funded primarily between local property taxes and state revenue.Through the state’s
36: funding formula, a school district receives a basic allotment for each student with weights added to that allotment
36: if the student receives additional services such as special education, at-risk (state compensatory education)
36: bilingual, dyslexia, early education, college, career or military readiness outcomes, career and technology and
36: transportation, which determines the district’s Tier I allotment.
36: The 2025-2026 General Fund budget, adopted in June 2025, included projected increase of $175.4 million in
36: revenues and $54.3 million in expenditures. The state revenue projections are based on an estimated average
36: daily attendance (ADA) of 151,666 or 223,312 based on a weighted average daily attendance (WADA).
36: In FY2025-2026, HISD’s total expenditures are estimated at $2.121 billion.
36: Actions of the 88th Legislature in 2025:
36: 1.The basic allotment increased to $6,215 for 2025-2026 and 2026-2027. This base amount is generated by
36: every student in HISD.
36: 2.Increased school safety funding from $10 per ADA to $20 per ADA. Added additional safety funding of
36: $33,540 per campus
36: 3.Increased the state homestead exemption from $100,000 to $140,000 which was subsequently approved by
36: voters in November 2025.
36: 4.Reduced the Maximum Compression Rate (MCR) by an additional $0.0194 for tax year 2025, subject to the
36: 90% equity band.
36: 5.Included Maintenance and Operations and Interest Sinking Hold Harmless (covers debt approved by voters
36: as of September 1, 2025.)
36: Tax Rate
36: At budget adoption in June, it was projected that the total district tax rate per $100 of property value would
36: be $0.8783. This assumed the Maintenance and Operations (M&O rate) of $0.7116 consisting of a maximum
36: compressed rate of $0.6322 plus five Tier II “Golden Pennies“ as well as an interest and sinking (I&S) rate of
36: $0.1667. These rates were approved by the Board of Managers in October.
36: CONTACTING THE DISTRICT’S FINANCIAL MANAGEMENT
36: This financial report is designed to provide our citizens, taxpayers, customers, investors, and creditors with a
36: general overview of the District’s finances and to demonstrate the District’s accountability for the money it receives.
36: If you have questions about this report or need additional financial information, contact the Chief Finance and
36: Operations Officer at 4400 West 18th Street, Houston, Texas 77092, or call (713) 556-5500.

42: Total Net Change in Fund Balances—Governmental Funds $ (324,881,876)
42: Amounts reported for governmental activities in the statement of activities are different because:
42: Governmental funds report capital outlays as expenditures. However, in the statement of activities,
42: the cost of those assets is allocated over their estimated useful lives as depreciation and amortization expense.
42: This is the amount by which capital assets changed in the current period.
42: Facilities acquisition and construction and other acquisitions 62,245,990
42: Disposals (26,294,651)
42: Depreciation expense (173,168,502)
42: Depreciation on disposals 22,584,143 (114,633,020)
42: Issuance of long-term debt provides current financial resources to governmental funds, but issuing debt
42: increases long-term liabilities in the statement of net position.
42: Issuance of refunding bonds at par (435,175,000)
42: Issuance of bond premiums (26,483,254)
42: Issuance of leases and subscriptions (14,876,089) (476,534,343)
42: Repayment of principal is an expenditure in the governmental funds, but the repayment reduces
42: long-term liabilities in the statement of net position. Lease and subscription terminations reduce
42: long-term liabilities in statement of net position, but are not current financial resources in the
42: governmental funds.
42: The change in long-term liabilities reported in governmental activities consists of the following:
42: Bonds payable 254,155,980
42: Notes payable 29,015,000
42: Leases payable 5,743,920
42: Subscription payable 9,478,343 298,393,243
42: Payment to the escrow agent to refund bonds from refunding proceeds reduces long-term liabilities. 452,294,020
42: Property taxes and other receivables, that will not be collected until after the District's fiscal year end,
42: are not considered "available" revenues and are deferred in the governmental funds. Deferred tax
42: revenues, net of allowance for bad debt, and other deferred revenues increased by this amount. 897,780
42: Interest on long-term debt in the statement of activities differs from the amount reported in the
42: governmental funds because interest is recognized as an expenditure in the funds when it is due,
42: and thus, requires the use of current financial resources. In the statement of activities, however
42: interest expense is recognized as the interest accrues, regardless of when it is due.

42: Amortization of premium on bonds payable 22,505,578
42: Amortization of deferred loss on refundings (724,837) 23,919,834

42: not require the use of current financial resources and, therefore, is not reported as expenditures
42: in the governmental fund 2,011,491

42: workers' compensation, health insurance, and print shop activities, to individual funds. The
42: net revenue (expense) of the internal service funds is reported with governmental activities. (34,835,805)

49: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
49: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
49: Notes to the Basic Financial Statements
49: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
49: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
49: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
49: NOTE 1: SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES
49: REPORTING ENTITY
49: The Houston Independent School District (“District”) is an independent school district created and
49: incorporated through legislation enacted by the Thirty-Eighth Texas State Legislature in 1923.
49: TheDistrictisthelargestschooldistrictinTexasandisgovernedbytheBoardofManagers,appointed
49: by the Texas Commissioner of Education in June 2023.
49: In accordance with GovernmentalAccounting Standards, a financial reporting entity consists of the
49: primary government and its component units. Component units are legally separate organizations
49: for which the elected officials of the District are financially accountable, or for which the relationship
49: to the District is such that exclusion would cause the District’s financial statements to be misleading
49: or incomplete. The District’s blended component unit, although a legally separate entity, is in
49: substance a part of the District’s operations, and so data from this entity is combined with data of
49: the primary government.
49: The criteria used to determine whether an organization is a component unit of the District includes:
49: financial accountability of the District for the component unit, whether the District appoints a voting
49: majority of the entity’s board, the ability to impose the District’s will on the component unit, fiscal
49: dependency criterion, if it is a financial benefit to or burden to the District, and whether services are
49: provided entirely or almost entirely to the District.
49: For financial reporting purposes, the Houston Independent School District GEAR UP Trust (“GEAR
49: UP Trust”) is included in the fiduciary activities of the District because of the financial accountability
49: of the District, the appointment of a voting majority, and the fiscal dependency criteria whereby the
49: GEAR UP Trust serves the District’s students exclusively. The GEAR UP Trust was incorporated
49: to ensure that the scholarship funds are properly spent and to guarantee a college scholarship to
49: the eligible students at participating schools of the GEAR UP program. The GEAR UP financial
49: information is blended with that of the District in the Private-Purpose Trust Funds column in the
49: StatementofFiduciaryNetPosition.SeparatefinancialstatementsforGEARUPTrustarenotissued.
49: IMPLEMENTATION OF NEW ACCOUNTING PRONOUNCEMENTS
49: GASB Statement No. 101, Compensated Absences (GASB 101), improves the information needs of
49: financial statements users by updating the recognition and measurement guidance for compensated
49: absences under a unified model and amending certain previously required disclosures. The
49: requirements of this statement are effective for reporting periods beginning after December 15, 2023,
49: with earlier application encouraged. GASB 101 was implemented in the District’s fiscal year 2025
49: financial statements with a restatement of ($12,281,689) to net position as of July 1, 2024 to reflect
49: the changes adopted to conform to the new standard.
49: GASB Statement No. 102, Certain Risk Disclosures (GASB 102), improves financial reporting by
49: providing users of financial statements with essential information regarding certain concentrations of
49: constraints and related events that have occurred or have begun to occur that make a government
49: vulnerabletoasubstantialimpact. Therequirementsofthisstatementareeffectiveforreportingperiods
49: beginning after June 15, 2024, with earlier application encouraged. GASB 102 was implemented
49: in the District’s fiscal year 2025 financial statements with no impact to amounts previously reported.
49: RECENT ACCOUNTING PRONOUNCEMENTS
49: GASB Statement No. 103, Financial Reporting Model Improvements (GASB 103), improves key
49: components of the financial reporting model to enhance its effectiveness in providing information
49: that is essential for decision making and assessing a government’s accountability. This statement
49: also addresses certain application issues. The requirements of this statement are effective for
49: reporting periods beginning after June 15, 2025, with earlier application encouraged. GASB 103
49: will be implemented in the District’s fiscal year 2026 financial statements and the impact has not yet
49: been determined.

50: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
50: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
50: GASBStatementNo.104,DisclosureofCertainCapitalAssets(GASB104),establishesrequirements
50: for certain types of capital assets to be disclosed separately in the capital assets note disclosures.
50: It also establishes requirements for capital assets held for sale, including additional disclosures
50: for those capital assets. The requirements of this statement are effective for reporting periods
50: beginning after June 15, 2025, with earlier application encouraged. GASB 104 will be implemented
50: in the District’s fiscal year 2026 financial statements and the impact has not yet been determined.
50: GOVERNMENT-WIDE AND FUND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
50: The Statement of Net Position and the Statement of Activities of the government-wide financial
50: statements report information on all the nonfiduciary activities of the District and its blended
50: component unit. The District eliminates from the Statement of Net Position and the Statement of
50: Activities most interfund receivables, payables, and transfers between funds. Interfund services
50: provided and used are eliminated in the process of consolidation. Governmental activities, which
50: normally are supported by taxes and intergovernmental revenues, are reported separately from
50: business-type activities, which rely to a significant extent on fees and charges for support.
50: The Statement ofActivities demonstrates the degree to which the direct expenses of a given function
50: are offset by program revenues. Program revenues include (1) charges to customers or applicants
50: for goods or services provided, and (2) grants and contributions that are restricted to meeting
50: operational requirements of a particular function. Program revenues included in the Statement of
50: Activities reduce the cost of the function to be financed from general revenues. Taxes and other
50: items not restricted to specific programs are properly excluded from program revenues and reported
50: instead as general revenues.
50: The District reports all direct expenses by function in the Statement of Activities. Direct expenses
50: are those clearly identifiable with a function. Depreciation and amortization expense is specifically
50: identified by function and is included in the direct expense to each function.
50: Separate financial statements are provided for governmental funds, proprietary funds, and fiduciary
50: funds. In accordance with the provisions of GASB Statement No. 34, the fiduciary funds are excluded
50: from the government-wide financial statements. Major individual governmental funds are reported
50: as separate columns in the fund financial statements.
50: MEASUREMENT FOCUS, BASIS OF ACCOUNTING
50: AND FINANCIAL STATEMENT PRESENTATION
50: Government-wide Financial Statements - The government-wide financial statements are reported
50: using the economic resources measurement focus and the accrual basis of accounting. Revenues,
50: expenses, gains, losses, assets, deferred outflows of resources, liabilities, and deferred inflows
50: of resources resulting from exchange and exchange-like transactions are recognized when the
50: exchange takes place, regardless of the timing of the related cash flows. Revenues, expenses,
50: gains, losses, assets, deferred outflows of resources, liabilities, and deferred inflows of resources
50: resulting from nonexchange transactions are recognized in accordance with GASB Statement No.
50: 33, as amended. Property taxes are recognized as revenues in the year for which they are levied.
50: Grants, and similar items, are recognized as revenue as soon as all eligibility requirements have
50: been met and funds are available to meet current expenditures.
50: Fund Financial Statements - Governmental fund financial statements are reported using the current
50: financial resources measurement focus and the modified accrual basis of accounting. Revenues
50: are recognized as soon as they are both measurable and available. State aid and miscellaneous
50: revenues, other than property taxes and state and federal grants, are considered to be available
50: if collected within the current period or within 90 days of the fiscal year end to pay liabilities of
50: the current period. Property taxes are considered to be available if collected within 60 days of the
50: fiscal year end. State and federal grants are considered to be available if collected within 120 days
51: - 41 -
51: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
51: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
51: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
51: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
51: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
51: of the fiscal year end. Expenditures are generally recognized under the modified accrual basis of
51: accounting in the accounting period in which the fund liability is incurred, if measurable. However,
51: debt service expenditures are recognized in the current fiscal period for debt service principal and
51: interest payments due within 30 days in the subsequent fiscal period as these funds have been set
51: aside for that purpose. Compensated absences and claims and judgments are recorded only when
51: payment is due. Both Proprietary fund statements and Fiduciary fund statements are reported using
51: the economic resources measurement focus and the accrual basis of accounting.
51: FUNDS
51: The District’s accounting system is organized and operated with the use of funds, each of which
51: is a separate accounting entity with a self-balancing set of accounts. The District’s resources are
51: allocated to and accounted for in individual funds based on the intended purposes for spending
51: and the means by which spending activities are controlled. The following fund types are included
51: in the financial statements:
51: Governmental Funds
51: Governmental Funds are those through which most governmental functions of the District are
51: financed. The acquisition, use, and balances of the District’s expendable financial resources, and
51: the related liabilities are accounted for through governmental funds. The following are the District’s
51: major governmental funds:
51: • General Fund - The General Fund is the general operating fund of the District and accounts
51: for all revenues and expenditures of the District not encompassed within other funds.
51: • Special Revenue Fund - The Special Revenue Fund is used to account for and report the
51: proceeds of specific revenue sources that are restricted or committed to expenditures for
51: specified purposes other than debt service or capital projects. The District’s Special Revenue
51: Fund includes the National School Breakfast and Lunch Program, United States Department
51: of Education grants, Campus Activity Funds, and all other federal, state and locally funded
51: grantsandprograms.ThesegrantsareawardedtotheDistrictforthepurposeofaccomplishing
51: specific educational tasks as defined in the grant awards. The schedules of federal and state
51: awards are included in the compliance section of theAnnual Comprehensive Financial Report.
51: • Debt Service Fund - The Debt Service Fund is used to account for the accumulation
51: of resources for, and the retirement of, general long-term debt and related costs.
51: • Capital Project Fund - The Capital Project Fund is used to account for financial resources
51: to be used for the acquisition, renovation, or construction of capital facilities.
51: Proprietary Funds
51: Proprietary Funds are used to account for operations that are financed in a manner similar to those
51: found in the private sector, where the determination of net income is appropriate for sound financial
51: administration.
51: • Enterprise Funds - The Enterprise Funds are used to account for operations that are financed
51: and operated similar to a private enterprise where the District’s intent is to provide services
51: financed primarily through user charges. The District’s enterprise funds include Medicaid and
51: the Marketplace activities.
51: • Internal Service Funds - The Internal Service Funds are used to account for operations
51: related to the Print Shop, Alternative Certification Program, Athletics, University
51: Interscholastic League (“UIL”), Shared Services, and risk financing activities of the District.
52: - 42 -
52: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
52: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
52: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
52: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
52: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
52: Proprietary funds distinguish operating revenues and expenses from nonoperating items.
52: Operating revenues and expenses generally result from providing services and producing
52: and delivering goods in connection with a proprietary fund’s principal ongoing operations.
52: The principal operating revenues of the District’s proprietary funds are charges to customers
52: and other funds for sales and services. Operating expenses for proprietary funds include the
52: cost of sales and services, administrative expenses, and depreciation on capital assets. All
52: revenues and expenses not meeting this definition are reported as nonoperating revenues
52: and expenses.
52: Fiduciary Funds
52: Fiduciary Funds are used to account for assets held by the District in a trustee capacity or as a
52: custodian for individuals, private organizations and/or other funds.
52: • Private-Purpose Trust Funds - Private-purpose trust funds are used to account for resources
52: legally held in trust under which principal and income benefit individuals, private organizations
52: or other governments. The District’s private-purpose trust funds represent scholarship funds
52: being held in trust for students.
52: • Custodial Funds - Custodial funds are used to account for resources that the District has
52: custody of, but is not the district’s own-source revenue, and does not control the use of
52: revenues. The District’s custodial funds represent school class and club activity funds.
52: Management’s Use of Estimates
52: The preparation of financial statements in conformity with generally accepted accounting principles
52: requires management to make estimates and assumptions that affect certain reported amounts
52: and disclosures. Actual results could differ from those estimates.
52: Stewardship, Compliance, and Accountability
52: The District is required by law to prepare and file a budget with the Texas EducationAgency (TEA).
52: Activities of the General Fund, Nutrition Services Fund, and Debt Service Fund are included in the
52: District’s formally adopted budget. Budgets for the Special Revenue Fund (other than Nutrition
52: Services) and Capital Project Fund are approved on a project basis. The District is required to
52: present the adopted and final amended budgeted revenues and expenditures and actual revenues
52: and expenditures for the General Fund, Nutrition Services Fund, and Debt Service Fund. These
52: budgets are in the financial section of the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report.
52: Pensions
52: The fiduciary net position of the Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) has been determined
52: using the flow of economic resources measurement focus and full accrual basis of accounting.
52: This includes for purposes of measuring the net pension liability, deferred outflows of resources
52: and deferred inflows of resources related to pensions, pension expense, and information about
52: assets, liabilities, and additions to/deductions from TRS’s fiduciary net position. Benefit payments
52: (including refunds of employee contributions) are recognized when due and payable in accordance
52: with the benefit terms. Investments are reported at fair value.
52: Other Post-Employment Benefits (OPEB)
52: The fiduciary net position of the Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) Care Plan has been
52: determined using the flow of economic resources measurement focus and full accrual basis of
52: accounting. This includes for purposes of measuring the net OPEB liability, deferred outflows of
52: resources and deferred inflows of resources related to other post-employment benefits, OPEB
52: expense, and information about assets, liabilities, and additions to/deductions from TRS Care’s
52: fiduciary net position. Benefit payments are recognized when due and payable in accordance with
52: the benefit terms. There are no investments as this is a pay-as-you-go plan and all cash is held in
52: a cash account.

53: ASSETS, LIABILITIES, AND DEFERRED OUTLFOWS AND INFLOWS OF RESOURCES
53: Cash and Cash Equivalents
53: The District requires all deposits to be fully collateralized with depository insurance; obligations
53: of the United States of America or its agencies and instrumentalities (excluding those mortgage
53: backed securities prohibited by the Public Funds Investment Act). At all times, such securities are
53: to have a fair value sufficient to fully collateralize deposit balances as adjusted by the amount of
53: applicable depository insurance pursuant to the Collateral for Public Funds Act, Texas Government
53: Code Section 2257.022.
53: The District considers cash and cash equivalents to be cash on hand and demand deposits. All
53: other monetary assets are treated as investments including certificates of deposit, investment pools,
53: money market instruments, and other securities defined under the Public Funds Investment Act.
53: Investments
53: Investments are stated at fair value where applicable under the GASB statements, which is the
53: amount at which the investment can be exchanged in a current transaction between willing parties.
53: Management of the District believes that in the areas of investment practice, management reports,
53: and establishment of appropriate policies, the District adhered to the requirements of the State
53: of Texas Public Funds Investment Act. Additionally, management of the District believes that
53: investment practices of the District were in accordance with local policies.
53: Receivables
53: All trade and property tax receivables are shown net of allowance for uncollectible balances. The
53: property tax receivable allowance is 52.6 percent of outstanding property taxes at June 30, 2025.
53: Inventories
53: Under the moving average inventory method, the average cost of each inventory item in stock is
53: recalculated after every inventory purchase. This method tends to yield inventory valuations and
53: cost of goods sold that are in-between those derived under the first in, first out (FIFO) method
53: and the last in, last out (LIFO) method. These materials and supplies are subsequently charged
53: to expenditures when consumed. Inventories include instructional materials, plant maintenance
53: operatingsupplies,aswellasnutritionservicesfoodandsupplies.Revenuefordonatedcommodities
53: is recognized when the commodities are received. Non-food inventories are offset at fiscal year-
53: end by non-spendable fund balance in the governmental fund financial statements.
53: Other Assets
53: Other assets include accrued interest, prepaid insurance, and prepaid travel. Prepaids are charged
53: to expenditures when consumed and are offset at year end by non-spendable fund balance in the
53: governmental fund financial statements.

54: Capital Assets
54: Capital assets, which include land, land improvements, buildings and improvements, construction
54: in progress, furniture and equipment, library books and media, vehicles, and right-to-use assets are
54: reported in the applicable governmental and business-type activities columns in the government-
54: wide financial statements and proprietary fund financial statements. Capital acquisition and
54: construction are reflected as expenditures in governmental funds. Land, land improvements,
54: buildings and improvements, furniture and equipment, and vehicles are recorded at historical
54: cost if purchased or constructed. The capitalization threshold is a unit cost of $5,000. Donated
54: capital assets are recorded at estimated acquisition value at the date of donation. Depreciation or
54: amortization on all depreciable capital assets begins on the date the asset is placed into service
54: using the straight-line method of depreciation.
54: The costs of normal maintenance and repairs that do not add to the value of the asset or materially
54: extend the life of the asset are not capitalized. Major outlays for capital assets and improvements
54: are capitalized as projects are constructed.
