55: Leases
55: The District is a lessee for noncancellable leases of property and equipment.The District recognizes
55: a lease liability, reported with long-term debt, and a right-to-use lease asset (lease asset), reported
55: with other capital assets, in the government-wide and proprietary fund financial statements.
55: The District recognizes lease liabilities with an initial, individual value of $5,000 or more.
55: At the commencement of a lease, the District initially measures the lease liability at the present
55: value of payments expected to be made during the lease term. Subsequently, the lease liability is
55: reduced by the principal portion of lease payments made. The lease asset is initially measured as
55: the initial amount of the lease liability, adjusted for lease payments made at or before the lease
55: commencement date, plus certain initial direct costs. Subsequently, the lease asset is amortized on
55: a straight-line basis over the shorter of the lease term or its useful life.
55: Key estimates and judgments related to leases include how the District determines (1) the discount
55: rate it uses to discount the expected lease payments to present value, (2) lease term, and (3) lease
55: payments.
55: The District uses the interest rate charged by the lessor as the discount rate. When the interest
55: rate charged by the lessor is not provided, the District generally uses its estimated incremental
55: borrowing rate as the discount rate for leases.
55: The lease term includes the noncancellable period of the lease.
55: Lease payments included in the measurement of the lease liability are composed of fixed payments,
55: variable payments fixed in substance or that depend on an index or a rate, purchase option price
55: that the District is reasonably certain to exercise, lease incentives receivable from the lessor, and
55: any other payments that are reasonably certain of being required based on an assessment of all
55: relevant factors.
55: The District monitors changes in circumstances that would require a remeasurement of its leases
55: and will remeasure the lease asset and liability if certain changes occur that are expected to
55: significantly affect the amount of the lease liability.
55: Subscription-Based Information Technology Agreements (SBITAs)
55: The District has noncancellable contracts with SBITA vendors for the right to use information
55: technology (IT) software, alone or in combination with tangible capital assets (the underlying IT
55: assets). The District recognizes a subscription liability, reported with long-term debt, and a right-to-
55: use subscription asset (an intangible asset), reported with other capital assets, in the government-
55: wide and proprietary fund financial statements. The District recognizes subscription liabilities with
55: an initial, individual value of $5,000 or more.
56: - 46 -
56: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
56: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
56: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
56: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
56: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
56: At the commencement of an SBITA, the District initially measures the subscription liability at the
56: present value of payments expected to be made during the subscription term. Subsequently, the
56: subscription liability is reduced by the principal portion of SBITA payments made. The subscription
56: asset is initially measured as the initial amount of the subscription liability, adjusted for SBITA
56: payments made at or before the SBITA commencement date, plus certain initial implementation
56: costs. Subsequently, the subscription asset is amortized on a straight-line basis over the shorter of
56: the subscription term or the useful life of the underlying IT assets.
56: Key estimates and judgments related to SBITAs include how the District determines (1) the discount
56: rate it uses to discount the expected subscription payments to present value, (2) subscription term,
56: and (3) subscription payments.
56: The District uses the interest rate charged by the SBITA vendor as the discount rate. When the
56: interest rate charged by the SBITA vendor is not provided, the District generally uses its estimated
56: incremental borrowing rate as the discount rate for SBITAs.
56: The subscription term includes the noncancellable period of the SBITA.
56: Subscription payments included in the measurement of the subscription liability are composed
56: of fixed payments, variable payments fixed in substance or that depend on an index or a rate,
56: termination penalties if the District is reasonably certain to exercise such options, subscription
56: contract incentives receivable from the SBITAvendor, and any other payments that are reasonably
56: certain of being required based on an assessment of all relevant factors.
56: The District monitors changes in circumstances that would require a remeasurement of its SBITAs
56: and will remeasure the subscription asset and liability if certain changes occur that are expected to
56: significantly affect the amount of the subscription liability.
56: Interfund Transactions
56: Activities between funds that are representative of lending/borrowing arrangements outstanding at
56: theendofthefiscalyeararereferredtoas“dueto/fromotherfunds.”Allotherbalancesbetweenfunds
56: are reported as transfers.Any residual balances outstanding between the governmental activities and
56: business-type activities are reported in the government-wide statements as “internal balances.”
56: A description of the basic types of interfund transactions and the related accounting policies are
56: as follows:
56: • Charges for services are reported as revenues for the performing fund and expenditures/
56: expenses of the requesting fund.
56: • Transactions to reimburse a fund for expenditures made by it for the benefit of another fund
56: are recorded as expenditures or expenses in the reimbursing fund and as a reduction of
56: expenditures or expenses in the fund that is reimbursed.
56: • Allotherinterfundtransfers,suchaslegallyauthorizedtransfersfromafundreceivingrevenue
56: to the fund through which the resources are to be expended, are transfers. Transfers are
56: classified as other financing sources or uses in the Statement of Revenues, Expenditures,
56: and Changes in Fund Balances.
56: • For reporting at the government-wide financial statement level, the District eliminates direct
56: interfund charges for services and the balances created within the same activity categories
56: (i.e. governmental vs. business-type). This process ensures neither governmental nor
56: business-type activities report direct internal revenue/expenditures. Interfund activity and
56: balances resulting from transactions with the fiduciary funds are not eliminated. Instead, the
56: fiduciary interfund activity and balances are treated as transactions with an external party.
56: The Internal Service Funds are essentially repositories for income, expenses, assets and
56: liabilities of the District’s health insurance, worker’s compensation, print shop, alternative
56: certification, athletics, shared services, and UIL activities.
57: - 47 -
57: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
57: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
57: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
57: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
57: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
57: Compensated Absences
57: The District’s contract employees earn one day of personal leave per month for all months under
57: contract, which may either be taken or accumulated with no limitation. Accumulated sick leave
57: balances are paid upon termination from the District to employees meeting certain criteria and hired
57: before October 10, 1972. In addition, the District may “buy-back” accrued but unused state leave in
57: accordance with the “Attendance Incentive Plan.”
57: Twelve-month employees earn vacation ranging from two to four weeks per year based on length
57: of service. Beginning with FY 2021, vacation days may be carried over from one contract year to
57: another with a maximum limit of 50 days compared to previous years when only 30 days could
57: be carried over. For twelve-month employees, the contract year runs from September 1st through
57: August 31st.
57: The District accrues vacation, state personal leave, and eligible sick leave in the government-wide
57: and proprietary fund financial statements. A liability and related expenditure for these amounts is
57: reported in the governmental funds only if they have matured as a result of employee retirements
57: or resignations and are due.
57: In June 2022, the Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) Statement No. 101,
57: Compensated Absences, effective for the fiscal year ending June 30, 2025, established updated
57: recognitionandmeasurementcriteriaforliabilitiesassociatedwithcompensatedabsencesincluding
57: vacation, sick leave, and other paid time off. A liability for compensated absences is recognized
57: when all the following conditions are met:
57: • The leave is attributable to services already rendered.
57: • The leave accumulates and carries forward to future periods.
57: • The leave is more likely than not to be used for time off or otherwise paid or settled.
57: The implementation of GASB 101 resulted in a change in accounting principle.As a result, previous
57: unrecognized liabilities for certain types of leave that meet the new recognition criteria have been
57: recorded. The cumulative effect of this change has been reflected as a restatement of beginning
57: net position in the government-wide financial statements.
57: The liabilities are measured using the pay rates in effect at the financial statement date or the
57: expected pay rates at the time of settlement. The District used 5 years of historical data and
57: current employment policies to estimate the likelihood of leave usage. The accrued types of leave
57: considered in the calculation was vacation, state personal leave, local personal leave, supplemental
57: sick leave, and compensatory time earned.
57: Long-term Liabilities
57: In the government-wide financial statements, long-term debt and other long-term obligations are
57: reported as liabilities in the Statement of Net Position. Bond premiums and discounts are amortized
57: over the life of the bonds. Gains and losses on bond refundings are amortized using the shorter of
57: the life of the refunded or refunding using the debt effective interest method. Bond issuance costs
57: are expensed as incurred.
57: In the fund financial statements, governmental funds recognize bond premiums and discounts,
57: as well as bond issuance costs, during the current period. The face amount of the debt issued
57: is reported as other financing sources. Premiums received on debt issuances are reported as
57: other financing sources while discounts on debt issuances are reported as other financing uses.
57: Issuance costs, whether or not withheld from the actual debt proceeds received, are reported as
57: debt service expenditures.
58: - 48 -
58: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
58: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
58: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
58: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
58: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
58: Deferred Outflows and Inflows of Resources
58: In addition to assets, the Statement of Net Position includes a separate section for deferred outflows
58: of resources which represents a consumption of net assets that applies to a future period(s) and
58: therefore, will not be recognized as an outflow of resources (expenses/expenditures) until then.
58: Included in this category are deferred losses on refunding,TRS pension costs, andTRS OPEB costs.
58: In addition to liabilities, the Statement of Net Position includes a separate section for deferred inflows
58: of resources which represents an acquisition of net assets that applies to a future period(s) and
58: therefore, will not be recognized as an inflow of resources (revenue) until then. Included in this
58: category are deferred gain on refunding,TRS pension and OPEB.The Governmental Funds Balance
58: Sheet also includes a section for deferred inflows of resources. Unavailable revenue is reported as
58: deferred inflows of resources only in the Governmental Funds Balance Sheet.
58: NET POSITION AND FUND BALANCES
58: Government-wide Financial Statements
58: The District’s net position is composed of the following:
58: Net Investment in Capital Assets - the component of net position that reports capital assets less
58: both the accumulated depreciation and amortization and the outstanding balance of debt that is
58: directly attributable to the acquisition, construction, or improvement of these capital assets.
58: Restricted for Debt Service - the component of net position that reports the difference between
58: assets and liabilities of the Debt Service Fund adjusted on a government-wide basis that consists
58: of assets with constraints placed on their use by the bond covenants.
58: Restricted for Grants - the component of net position that reports the difference between assets
58: and liabilities of the Special Revenue Fund that consists of assets with constraints placed on their
58: use by the Department of Education, Department of Agriculture, TEA, and other grantor agencies
58: and organizations.
58: Unrestricted - - the difference between the assets and liabilities that are not reported in net position
58: invested in capital assets, net position restricted for debt service, and net position restricted for grants.
58: The District applies restricted resources before unrestricted resources when an expense is incurred
58: for which restricted net position is available.
58: Governmental Fund Financial Statements
58: Inthefundfinancialstatements,governmentalfundsreportfundbalancesaseitheranon-spendable
58: fund balance or a spendable fund balance.
58: Non-spendable Fund Balance
58: Non-spendable fund balance is that portion of fund balance that is not expendable (such as
58: inventories) or is legally earmarked for a specific use. Non-spendable fund balance reserves
58: include inventories and prepaid items.
58: Spendable Fund Balance
58: Spendable fund balance is the portion of fund balance that is comprised of restricted, committed,
58: assigned, and unassigned fund balances.
58: Restricted Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance constrained to a specific
58: purpose by the provider, such as a grantor. Restricted fund balance includes funds for the Nutrition
58: Servicesprogram,fundsrestrictedforconstructionprograms,fundsusedtoretiredebt,andresources
58: from other granting agencies.
59: - 49 -
59: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
59: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
59: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
59: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
59: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
59: Committed Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance constrained to a specific
59: purpose by a resolution approved by the Board. Those committed amounts cannot be used for any
59: otherpurposeunlesstheBoardremovesorchangesthespecifiedusebytakingthesameactionused
59: to establish it. Committed fund balance includes funds for potential litigation, claims, and judgments.
59: Assigned Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance that is earmarked for a
59: specific purpose by the Superintendent or Chief Financial Officer. The assigned amounts can only be
59: removed by the Superintendent or Chief Financial Officer. Assigned fund balance includes funds for
59: encumbrances which are purchase orders, contracts, and other commitments for the expenditure of
59: funds that are carried forward, insurance deductibles, specific program costs, and other legal issues.
59: Unassigned Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance within the General Fund
59: which may be spent for any legal purpose. This portion of the total fund balance in the general fund is
59: available to finance operating expenditures. Per Board Policy, the District’s unassigned fund balance
59: at year end shall equal at least three months of operating expenditures.
59: In general, it is the District’s policy to consider restricted resources to have been spent first when
59: an expenditure is incurred for purposes for which restricted and unrestricted (i.e. committed,
59: assigned, or unassigned) fund balances are available, followed by committed and then assigned
59: fund balances. Unassigned amounts are used only after the other resources have been used.

59: NOTE 2: HISD PUBLIC FACILITY CORPORATION
59: TheHoustonIndependentSchoolDistrictPublicFacilityCorporationisapublicnon-profitcorporation,
59: the creation of which was sponsored by the District pursuant to the Public Facility Corporation Act,
59: Article 717s, Vernon’s Texas Civil Statutes, as amended (the “Public Facility Corporation Act”).
59: The Public Facility Corporation Act authorizes the creation and utilization by school districts of
59: public facility corporations to issue bonds to provide for the acquisition, construction, rehabilitation,
59: renovation, repair, equipping, furnishing and placing in service of public facilities of its governmental
59: sponsor and further authorizes the governmental sponsor to incur lease purchase obligations in
59: favor of the corporation to serve as security for the bonds issued by the Public Facility Corporation.

60: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

60: To accomplish the financings, the Public Facility Corporation and the District have entered into a
60: number of “Leases With An Option to Purchase” pursuant to the terms of which the District will pay
60: to the Public Facility Corporation such lease payments at such times and in such amounts as will
60: be required to pay the principal and premium, if any, and interest on the lease revenue bonds as
60: they become due.
60: The Public Facility Corporation has utilized this structure to issue Lease Revenue Bonds for building
60: two high schools in 1998, a food warehouse facility in 2006, four elementary schools in 2010 and
60: three instructional facilities in 2015. The Public Facility Corporation refunded the Series 2006 (food
60: warehouse) bonds in fiscal year 2017 for the purpose of decreasing debt service costs in future
60: years. In fiscal year 2020 the Public Facility Corporation refunded the Series 2010B (BAB) bonds
60: for the purpose of decreasing debt service costs in future years. In fiscal year 2023, Series 2014A-
60: 1B bonds were refunded for the purpose of decreasing debt services costs in future years.
60: NOTE 3: CASH DEPOSITS AND INVESTMENTS
60: Statutes of the State of Texas and policies mandated by the District’s School Board authorize the
60: District to invest in obligations of the U.S. Government or its agencies, repurchase agreements,
60: commercial paper, corporate bonds, public fund investment pools, mutual funds and money market
60: accounts. All investments are held separately in each of the funds. All bank demand accounts are
60: recorded within the General Fund.
60: With the exception of one occasion, a three day period during the weekend of September 6 - 8, 2024,
60: District funds held at the depository bank were properly secured during the fiscal year ended June 30,
60: 2025. During September 6 - 8, 2024, District funds were undercollateralized by $1,390,337.
60: Cash, Demand and Time Deposits
60: As of June 30, 2025, the carrying amount of the District’s demand deposits and cash on hand was
60: $12,823,546. The $ 25,099,585 bank balance was fully covered on June 30, 2025 by a combination
60: offederaldepositinsurancethroughtheFDICandcollateralizationbysecuritiesheldbytheDistrict’s
60: depository in joint safekeeping at the Federal Reserve Bank in the District’s name.
60: Investments
60: The District’s investments in public funds investment pools and money market mutual funds at June
60: 30, 2025 include those with the Lone Star Investment Pool (“LSIP”), TexPool, Texas-CLASS, Texas
60: Range, and Texas Fixed Income Trust (TX-FIT). The value of the District’s position in the above
60: pools is the same as the value of the pool shares, which are measured at amortized cost or net
60: asset value (NAV).
60: LSIPisapublicfundsinvestmentpoolcreatedpursuanttotheInterlocalCooperationActoftheState
60: of Texas, Article 4413(32c), Vernon’s Texas Civil Statutes, as amended, and is designed to provide
60: participating local governments with investment vehicles for local funds that may be required for
60: immediate expenditure. The primary objective of the LSIP is to emphasize the importance of safety
60: of principal and liquidity of pool assets. The administrator and distributor of LSIP is First Public,
60: LLC, a Texas limited liability company. Investment advisory services are provided by American
60: Beacon Advisors and Mellon Investments Corporation. The custodial account is managed by State
60: Street Bank.
60: The Lone Star Government Overnight Fund is a stable net asset value fund rated AAAm by
60: Standard & Poor’s rating service, Lone Star Corporate Overnight Fund is a stable net asset value
60: fund rated AAAm by Standard & Poor’s, and the Lone Star Corporate Overnight Plus Fund is a
60: stable net asset value rated AAAf/S1+ by Standard & Poor’s. All of these funds seek to maintain a
60: stable $1.00 net asset value.
60: The District’s investment in LSIP’s Corporate Overnight Fund, and the Corporate Overnight Plus
60: Fund, as of June 30, 2025 was $21,792 and $405,014,440 respectively, for a total of $405,036,232,
60: representing 30.58 percent of the total portfolio.

61: than 1 percent of the total portfolio.

61: Investment Act) of Texas Government Code. Texas Range is administered by PFM Asset
61: Management and all funds are held by the custodial agent, U.S. Bank, N.A.
61: TexasDAILY, rated AAAm by Fitch, is a local government investment pool that seeks to maintain a
61: stable $1.00 net asset value and offers daily liquidity. TexasTERM, rated AAAf by Fitch, is a fixed-
61: term investment portfolio with maturities up to one year.
61: As of June 30, 2025, the District’s investment in TexasDAILY was $25,391 and in TexasTERM was
61: $50,000,000, representing 3.78 percent of the total investment portfolio.
61: TX-FIT is a public funds investment pool designed to provide local governments with investment
61: vehicles for local funds that may be required for immediate expenditure. It emphasizes safety
61: of principal and liquidity of pool assets. Texas FIT is managed by U.S. Fixed Income Trust. The
61: value of the District’s position in the pool is the same as the value of the pool shares, measured
61: at amortized cost or net asset value (NAV). The TX-FIT Pools are currently rated by Fitch Ratings.
61: The TX-FIT Cash Pool is currently rated AAAf/S1. The TX-FIT Government Pool is currently rated
61: AAAmmf.
61: The TX-FIT investments in each of the pools within the Trust are measured and reported at fair
61: market value using the guidelines established by Generally Accepted Accounting Principles
61: (GAAP). The Trust follows Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) Accounting Standards
61: Topic (ASC) 820 Fair Value Measurement and Disclosure for financial reporting purposes.ASC 820
61: defines fair value, establishes a single framework for measuring fair value, and requires disclosures
61: about fair value measurement. TX-FIT does not meet all of the specific criteria outlined in GASB 79
61: paragraph 4, therefore TX-FIT investments are measured at fair value as provided in paragraph 11
61: of GASB Statement 31.
61: As of June 30, 2025, the District’s investment in Texas FIT was $591,721,940, representing 44.68
61: percent of the total portfolio.
62: - 52 -
62: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
62: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
62: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
62: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
62: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
62: GASB Statement No. 40, Deposit and Investment Risk Disclosures, an amendment to GASB
62: Statement No. 3, establishes and modifies disclosure requirements related to deposits and
62: investment risks: credit risk (including custodial credit risk and concentrations of credit risk), interest
62: rate risk, and foreign currency risk.
62: To ensure compliance with the various risk factors addressed in GASB Statement No. 40, the
62: Cash Management and Investment Policy is reviewed at least annually and presented to the
62: School Board for approval. The Investment Officers submit Quarterly Investment Reports to the
62: School Board certifying that all investments were purchased in compliance with the Public Fund
62: Investment Act.
62: The District’s Cash Management and Investment Policy contains the following objectives:
62: (1) assure the safety of District’s funds;
62: (2) maintain sufficient liquidity to provide adequate and timely working funds;
62: (3) maintenance of the public trust as custodians and managers responsible for the investing of
62: funds subject to state and federal laws;
62: (4) optimization of yield as expressed in terms of rate of return and interest earnings;
62: (5) diversification of investments as relating to maturity, instruments, and issuers shall be
62: considered a priority within the context of the overall investment policy; and
62: (6) avoid investments for speculation.
62: All objectives are approached with the judgment and care, under the circumstances then prevailing,
62: which persons of prudence, discretion, and intelligence exercise in the management of their own
62: affairs, not for speculation, but for investment, considering the safety of the capital, as well as
62: the probable income to be derived. Preservation of District capital is of highest importance. All
62: participants in the investment process seek to act responsibly as custodians of the public trust.
62: Investment officials avoid any transaction that might impair public confidence in the District’s ability
62: to govern effectively.
62: Interest Rate Risk
62: As a means of limiting its exposure to fair value losses that could occur from rising interest rates,
62: the District’s investment policy prohibits any investment for speculative gains. The available
62: funds in the General Fund may be invested for greater than one year provided that all cash flow
62: requirements have been met. Bond proceeds can be invested for a period up to five years provided
62: that the drawdown schedules permit such maturities.
62: The District recognizes that investment risks can result from market price changes to mitigate the
62: risk, all securities are purchased with the intention of holding to maturity. Portfolio diversification
62: is employed as a way to control risk. Investment officers are expected to display prudence in the
62: selection of securities, as a way to minimize risk. No individual investment transaction shall be
62: undertaken which jeopardizes the total capital position of the overall portfolio.

64: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
64: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
64: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
64: GASB Statement Number 72 established a hierarchy that prioritizes the inputs used to measure fair
64: value. Certain investment types utilized by the District are not required to be measured at fair value.
64: The District has investments in TexPool Prime which maintains stable net asset values of $1 per
64: share and utilize the amortized cost method as allowed by GASB 79. The District has investments
64: in Texas FIT which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and measures fair value as provided
64: in paragraph 11 of GASB Statement 31. The District invests in the Lone Star Corporate Overnight
64: Fund which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and the underlying pool investments are
64: reported at amortized cost per GASB 79. The District invests in the Lone Star Corporate Overnight
64: Plus Fund which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and the pool utilizes fair value reporting
64: per GASB 72. The District invests in the Texas-CLASS fund which maintains a stable net asset
64: value of $1 and measures fair value as provided in paragraph 11 of GASB Statement 31. The
64: District invests in the Texas Range TexasDAILY Select fund which maintains a stable net asset
64: value of $1 and the underlying pool investments are reported at amortized cost per GASB 79. The
64: District has no unfunded commitments to these pools and may redeem investments at any time.
64: CustodialCreditRisk-InvestmentspurchasedfortheDistrictaremadeinthenameoftheHouston
64: Independent School District. All investments are purchased with the delivery versus payment
64: method and are recorded in the District’s name on the Federal Reserve’s book entry system and
64: confirmed to the District via safekeeping receipt maintained on the books of the depository bank’s
64: safekeeping department.
64: Concentration of Credit Risk - The investment portfolio is diversified to eliminate the risk of loss
64: resulting from over concentration of assets in a specific maturity, a specific issue, or a specific class
64: of securities. Within the cash flow requirement constraints, investment maturities are staggered in
64: a way that avoids undue concentration of assets in a specific maturity sector.
64: The District does not invest its portfolio in any single investment pool. Funds are subdivided
64: between various pools for diversification and security reasons.
64: The District is prohibited from investing in the following types of investments:
64: a. obligations whose payment represents the coupon payments on the outstanding principal
64: balance of the underlying mortgage-backed security collateral and pays no principal;
64: b. obligations whose payment represents the principal stream of cash flow from the underlying
64: mortgage-backed security collateral and bears no interest;
64: c. collateralized mortgage obligations that have a stated final maturity date of greater than
64: 10 years; and
64: d. collateralized mortgage obligations whose interest rate is determined by an index that
64: adjusts opposite to the changes in a market index.
64: The District neither enters into reverse repurchase agreements nor trades in options or future
64: contracts. Additionally, the District does not participate in any securities lending program.
64: Foreign Currency Risk
64: The District does not engage in any deposit or investment transactions involving foreign currency.
64: NOTE 4: PROPERTY TAXES AND STATE AID REVENUE
64: PROPERTY TAXES
64: The appraisal of property within the District is the responsibility of the Harris County Appraisal
64: District (“Appraisal District”). The District’s ad valorem property tax is levied annually in October
64: on the assessed value as of the prior January 1 for all real and business personal property located
64: in the District. Taxes are due on receipt of the tax bill and are delinquent if not paid by January
64: 31 of the next calendar year. Such taxes are applicable to the fiscal year in which they are levied
64: and become delinquent with an enforceable lien on property after January 31 of the subsequent
64: calendar year. Delinquent taxes receivable on the government-wide statement of net position and
64: the governmental fund balance sheet is net of estimated uncollectible taxes.

65: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
65: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
65: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
65: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
65: For the current fiscal year, the Board of Education set a tax rate of $0.8683 per $100 of assessed
65: valuation. The maintenance and debt service portions of such rate are $0.7016 and $0.1667
65: respectively. The 2024 tax year assessed valuation was $233,190,075,832 which resulted in a
65: gross tax levy of $2,024,789,682 for the current fiscal year. After deductions of all exemptions
65: and reductions provided by law and those granted by the District, the 2024 tax year levy was
65: $1,959,305,399 as of June 30, 2025.
65: Property taxes which are measurable (quantifiable) and available (collectible within the current
65: period or soon enough thereafter to finance expenditures of the current period, which the District
65: has estimated to be collected in the two months after the fiscal year end) are recognized as
65: revenue in the year of levy in the governmental fund financial statements. Property taxes, which are
65: measurable but not available, are recorded net of estimated uncollectible amounts, as unavailable
65: revenues in the year of the levy in the governmental financial statements. Such unavailable
65: revenues are recognized in the fund financial statements as revenue in the fiscal year in which they
65: become available. In the government-wide financial statements, property taxes are recognized
65: as revenues in the year for which the taxes are levied. Uncollectible property taxes in the current
65: period increased the allowance for uncollectible property taxes in the government-wide and fund
65: financial statements by $5,299,956.

65: fund financial statements as of June 30, 2025 are as follows:

65: of the contributions to the Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS). See Note 12 for additional
65: information on the employees’ retirement plan. Other state revenues are received through other
65: state miscellaneous programs on an allocated basis. The components of state aid as shown in the

71: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
71: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
71: The following is a summary of the District’s transfers for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025:
71: $16,215,400
71: From General Fund to the Debt Service Fund for interest and principal
71: debt payments
71: $4,762,500
71: From the Capital Project Fund to the Debt Service Fund for TIRZ High
71: School and Elementary School debt payments
71: $11,000,000 From the Capital Project Fund to the General Fund for capital projects.
71: $9,000,000
71: From the Nonmajor Medicaid Enterprise Fund to the General Fund for
71: use of unrestricted revenues. This is an annual transfer.
71: $12,359,204
71: From Health Insurance Fund to the Worker’s Compensation Fund to
71: cover premium costs.
71: $4,398,408 From the Campus Activity Fund to General Fund for Powerup funds.
71: $57,735,512 Total transfers
71: Transfers are used to (1) move revenues from the fund that statute or budget requires to collect
71: them to the fund that statute or budget requires to expend them, (2) move receipts restricted
71: to debt service from the funds collecting the receipts to the debt service fund as debt service
71: payments become due, (3) use unrestricted revenues collected in the general fund to finance
71: various programs accounted for in other funds in accordance with budgetary authorizations, and
71: (4) use unrestricted revenues collected in a proprietary fund to finance activities in the general fund
71: or another proprietary fund.
71: NOTE 8: LONG-TERM LIABILITIES AND RELATED DEFERRED
71: INFLOWS AND OUTFLOWS
71: The District’s long-term liabilities consist of bond and note indebtedness, leases and subscriptions,
71: health insurance, workers’ compensation, compensated absences, net pension liability and
71: net OPEB liability. The current requirements for general obligation bonds principal and interest
71: expenditures are accounted for in the debt service fund. Current funding requirements for leases,
71: subscriptions, health insurance, and workers’ compensation are accounted for in the governmental
71: and proprietary funds. Pension and OPEB long-term liabilities are generally liquidated with
71: resources of the general fund.

72: Internal Service Funds predominantly serve governmental funds. Accordingly, long-term liabilities

72: Debt Payable-Governmental Activities
72: At June 30, 2025, the District had $1,513,635,000 outstanding of general obligation and lease
72: revenue bonds, and maintenance tax notes. These debt obligations were issued at various dates
72: from November 1, 2009, through May 1, 2025, with interest rates ranging from 1.00 percent to
72: 6.17 percent, for the purpose of new construction, and equipment of facilities and maintenance of
72: facilities. All debt obligations fully mature at various dates through February 15, 2043. As of June
72: 30, 2025, the District had no authorized but unissued debt capacity.
72: Of the District’s $1,513,635,000 total debt outstanding at June 30, 2025, $27,505,000 of bonds
72: payable are attributable to lease revenue bonds issued by the Public Facility Corporation.
72: In current and prior years, the District legally defeased certain bonds by placing cash and/or
72: proceeds of refunding bond issues in an irrevocable trust to provide for all future debt service
72: payments on the refunded debt. Accordingly, the trust account assets and the liability for the
72: defeased bonds are not included in the District’s financial statements. As of June 30, 2025, there
72: were $115,420,000 in outstanding defeased bonds.
72: On May 28, 2025, the District defeased $52,705,000 in outstanding par on the Fixed Rate Limited
72: Tax Schoolhouse Bonds, Series 2017, that were sold with a final maturity date of February 15,
72: 2042. The District utilized available debt service funds for the full redemption of the bond series.

73: Also, on May 28 2025, the District defeased $62,715,000 of outstanding par on the Fixed Rate
73: Limited Tax Refunding Bonds, Series 2018, that were sold with a final maturity date of February 15,
73: 2043. The District utilized available debt service funds for the full redemption of the bond series.
73: On March 6, 2025, the District executed a targeted debt tender offer. This tender involved selected
73: maturities from the Series 2016A, 2017, and 2018 bonds, and was financed through the issuance
73: of $149,685,000 of Limited Tax Refunding Bond Series 2025A and $20,555,000 of Maintenance
73: Tax Refunding Note Series 2025 bonds, rather than utilizing available debt service funds. The total
73: principal amount refunded was $183,780,000, resulting in a debt service savings of $14,764,530
73: and an economic gain of $10,927,064.
73: On May 1, 2025, the District redeemed Variable Rate Limited Tax Refunding Bond Series 2014A-2,
73: Variable Rate Limited Tax Refunding Bond Series 2023C, and Fixed Rate Limited Tax Refunding
73: Bond Series 2014B bond and was financed through the issuance of Limited Tax Refunding Bond
73: Series 2025B. The total principal amount refunded was $268,655,000 resulting in a debt service
73: savings of $57,389,278, and an economic gain of $17,500,810.
73: As of June 30, 2025, the District had no outstanding variable rate unlimited tax school building bonds.

76: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
76: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
76: Other long-term debt
76: Federal Arbitrage - The Tax Reform Act of 1986 instituted certain arbitrage restrictions consisting
76: of complex regulations with respect to issuance of tax-exempt bond proceeds at an interest yield
76: greater than the interest yield paid to bondholders. Generally, all interest paid to bondholders can
76: be retroactively rendered taxable if applicable rebates are not reported and paid to the Internal
76: Revenue Service at least every five years for applicable bond issues. Accordingly, there is the risk
76: that if such calculations are not performed or are not performed correctly, a liability to the District
76: could result. The District periodically engages an arbitrage consultant to perform the calculations in
76: accordance with the Internal Revenue Service’s rules and regulations. The District did not identify
76: arbitrage liability as of June 30, 2025.
76: Compensated Absences - On retirement or death of certain employees hired before October 10,
76: 1972, the District pays any accumulated unused sick leave.Additionally, the District may “buy back”
76: any accrued but unused state leave from employees in accordance with the Attendance Incentive
76: Plan. Also, certain employees are entitled to receive accrued vacation pay in a lump-sum cash
76: payment upon termination of employment with the District. The General Fund has typically been
76: used in prior years to liquidate compensated absences.
76: NOTE 9: LEASES
76: The District has entered into multiple lease agreements as lessee. The leases allow the right-to-use
76: computers, copiers and other assets over the term of the lease. The District is required to make
76: annual payments on the laptop leases and monthly payments on all other leases at its incremental
76: borrowing rate or the interest rate stated or implied within the leases. The lease rate, term and
76: ending lease liability are as follows:

77: interest rate stated or implied within the agreement.

77: NOTE 11: RISK MANAGEMENT
77: The District is exposed to various risks of loss related to torts, theft of, damage to and destruction
77: of assets, errors and omissions, injuries to employees, and natural disasters. The District’s risk
77: management program encompasses various means of protecting the District against losses
77: through policies with commercial insurance carriers or through self-insurance.
77: Workers’ Compensation
77: The District maintains a self-insurance program for workers’ compensation. Contributions are
77: paid from all governmental and proprietary funds to the Workers’ Compensation Internal Service
77: Fund from which all claims and administrative expenses are paid. Claims administration, loss
77: control, and consultant services are provided by a third-party administrator. The District maintains
77: a catastrophic loss insurance policy for catastrophic losses exceeding $1,000,000 per occurrence
77: up to statutory limit of liability.

78: Health Insurance
78: Effective January 1, 2002, the District established a self-insurance program for health insurance.
78: Contributions are paid from all governmental and proprietary funds to the Health Insurance Internal
78: Service Fund from which all claims and administrative expenses are paid. Claims administration
78: and consultant services are provided by a third party administrator.The District maintains aggregate
78: stop loss coverage for medical cost.
78: The District is insured for covered medical paid and incurred during the plan year by Aetna under
78: an annual aggregate insurance contract. The coverage provides that the insurer will reimburse the
78: District for such paid claims that exceed an annual aggregate attachment point that is determined
78: by a monthly amount per covered employee based on the tier of coverage enrolled.
78: An accrual for incurred but not reported claims in the amount of $19,251,623, of which $19,951,623
78: is the current portion, has been recorded in the fund as of June 30, 2025. Claims payable, including
78: an estimate of claims incurred but not reported, was actuarially determined based on an estimate
78: of the remaining liability on known claims.

79: Property, Casualty, General and Professional Liability
79: The District purchasesAll-Risk Property Insurance with limits of $250,000,000. The policies include
79: sublimits of $100,000,000 per occurrence for flood, $75,000,000 for quake and $150,000,000 per
79: occurrence for Named Storms. General and Professional Liability risks are insured with limits of
79: $9,750,000. Within these policy limits, the District’s exposure for covered losses is limited to the
79: policy deductibles and self-insured retentions. Automobile liability exposures are self-insured in
79: Texas and insurance coverage is purchased for out of state risks with a $1,000,000 limit. The
79: District is self-insured for workers compensation and employer’s liability risk exposure. The District
79: has not had any claims in excess of its policy limits in the past three years.
79: NOTE 12: EMPLOYEES’ RETIREMENT PLAN
79: Pensions
79: Plan Description
79: The District participates in a cost-sharing multiple-employer defined benefit pension that has a
79: special funding situation. The plan is administered by the Teacher Retirement System of Texas
79: (TRS). It is a defined benefit pension plan established and administered in accordance with the
79: Texas Constitution, Article XVI, Section 67 and Texas Government Code, Title 8, Subtitle C. The
79: pension trust fund is a qualified pension trust under Section 401(a) of the Internal Revenue Code.
79: The Texas Legislature establishes benefits and contribution rates within the guidelines of the
79: Texas Constitution. The pension’s Board of Trustees does not have the authority to establish or
79: amend benefit terms. All employees of public, state-supported educational institutions in Texas
79: who are employed for one-half or more of the standard workload and who are not exempted from
79: membership under Texas Government Code, Title 8, Section 822.002 are covered by the system.

80: Benefit Provisions and Service Requirements
80: TRS administers retirement, disability, and death and survivor benefits to employees and
80: beneficiaries of employees of the public school system of Texas. The pension formula is calculated
80: using 2.3 percent times the average of the five highest annual creditable salaries times years of
80: creditedservicetoarriveattheannualstandardannuityexceptformemberswhoaregrandfathered,
80: the three highest annual salaries are used. Service requirements are as follows:
80: Normal -- Age 65 with 5 years of credited service, or when the sum of member’s age and years of
80: credit equals or exceeds 80.
80: Reduced --Age 55 with at least 5 years of credited service, or earlier than 55 with 30 or more years
80: of credited service.
80: Members are fully vested after five years of creditable service and are entitled to any benefit for
80: which eligibility requirements have been met.There are additional provisions for reduced retirement
80: if the sum of the member’s age and years of service credit total at least 80, but the member is less
80: than age 60 or 62 depending on date of employment, or if the member was grandfathered under
80: a previous rule. For more details about benefits see the Summary of Benefits section of TRS
80: 2024 Annual Comprehensive Financial Report. There are no automatic post-employment benefit
80: changes, including automatic COLAs. Ad hoc post-employment benefit changes, including ad hoc
80: COLAs can be granted by the Texas Legislature as noted in the Plan description. The 2023 Texas
80: Legislature passed Senate Bill (SB) 10 and House Joint Resolution (HJR) 2 to provide eligible
80: retirees with a one-time stipend and an ad hoc cost-of-living-adjustment (COLA).
80: One-Time Stipends
80: Stipends were paid in September 2023 to annuitants who met the qualifying age requirement on or
80: before August 31, 2023:
80: • A one-time $7,500 stipend to 75 years of age and older eligible annuitants
80: • A one-time $2,400 stipend to 70 to 74 years of age eligible annuitants

81: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
81: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
81: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
81: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
81: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
81: Cost-of-Living Adjustment
81: A cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) was dependent on Texas voters approving a constitutional
81: amendment (Proposition 9) to authorize the COLA. Voters approved the amendment in the
81: November 2023 election and the following COLA was applied to eligible annuitants’ payments
81: beginning with their January 2024 payment:
81: • 2% COLAfor eligible retirees who retired between September 1, 2013 throughAugust 31, 2020.
81: • 4% COLAfor eligible retirees who retired between September 1, 2001 throughAugust 31, 2013.
81: • 6% COLA for eligible retirees who retired on or before August 31, 2001.
81: Texas Government Code section 821.006 prohibits benefit improvements, if, as a result of the
81: particular action, the time required to amortizeTRS unfunded actuarial liabilities would be increased
81: to a period that exceeds 31 years, or, if the amortization period already exceeds 31 years, the
81: period would be increased by such action. Actuarial implications of the funding provided in the
81: manner are determined by the System’s actuary.
81: Contributions
81: Contribution requirements are established or amended pursuant to Article 16, section 67 of the
81: Texas Constitution which requires the Texas legislature to establish a member contribution rate
81: of not less than 6 percent of the member’s annual compensation and a state contribution of not
81: less than 6 percent and not more than 10 percent of the aggregate annual compensation paid to
81: members of the system during the fiscal year.
81: Employee contribution rates are set in state statute, Texas Government Code 825.402. The TRS
81: Pension Reform Bill (Senate Bill 12) of the 86th Texas Legislature amended Texas Government
81: Code 825.402 for member contributions and increased employee and employer contribution rates
81: for fiscal years 2019 through 2025.
81: Contributors to the plan include members, employers, and the State of Texas as the only non-
81: employer contributing entity. The State contributes to the plan in accordance with state statutes and
81: the General Appropriations Act (GAA).

82: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

82: As the non-employer contributing entity for public education, the State contributes to the retirement
82: system an amount equal to the current District’s contribution rate times the aggregate annual
82: compensation of all participating members of the pension trust fund during that fiscal year reduced
82: by the amounts described below which are paid by the District. The District is required to pay the
82: employer contribution rate in the following instances:
82: • On the portion of the member’s salary that exceeds the statutory minimum for members
82: entitled to the statutory minimum under Section 21.402 of the Texas Education Code
82: • During a new member’s first 90 days of employment
82: • When any part or all of an employee’s salary is paid by federal funding source, a privately
82: sponsored source, from non-educational and general, or local funds
82: In addition to the employer contributions listed above, the District is required to pay the following
82: surcharges:
82: • 1.9 percent of the member’s salary beginning in fiscal year 2024, gradually increasing
82: to 2 percent in fiscal year 2025
82: • When employing a retiree of the Teacher Retirement System, the District shall pay both the
82: member contribution and the state contribution as an employment after retirement surcharge

82: from active members, employers and the non-employer contributing entity will be made at the
82: statutorily required rates set during the 2019 session. It is assumed that future employer and state
82: contributions will be 9.54 percent of payroll in fiscal year 2025 and thereafter. This includes all
82: employer and state contributions for active and rehired retirees. Based on these assumptions,

84: Pension Liabilities, Pension Expense, and Deferred Outflows of Resources
84: and Deferred Inflows of Resources Related to Pensions
84: On June 30, 2025, the District reported a net pension liability of $767,987,153 for its proportionate
84: share of the TRS’s net pension liability. The amount recognized by the District as its proportionate

85: Changes since the Prior Measurement Date
85: The actuarial assumptions and methods are the same as used in the determination of prior year’s
85: net pension liability.
85: The 2023 Texas Legislature passed Senate Bill 10 (SB10), which provided a stipend payment to
85: certain retirees and variable ad hoc cos-of-living adjustments (COLA) to certain retirees in early
85: fiscal year 2024. Due to its timing, the legislation and payments were not reflected in the August
85: 31, 2023 actuarial valuation. Under the roll forward method, an adjustment was made to reflect
85: the legislation in the rolled forward liabilities for the current measurement year, August 31, 2024.
85: SB10 and House Joint Resolution 2 (HJR 2) of the 88th Regular Legislative Session appropriated
85: payments of $1.645 billion for one-time stipends and $3.355 billion for COLAs. This appropriation
85: is treated as a supplemental contribution and included in other additions. Since the Legislature
85: appropriated funds for this one-time stipend and COLA, there was no impact on the net pension
85: liability of TRS.

86: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
86: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
86: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
86: Other Post-Employment Benefit Plans
86: Health Care Plan Description (TRS-Care)
86: The District participates in the Texas Public School Retired Employees Group Insurance Program
86: (TRS-Care). It is a multiple-employer, cost-sharing defined Other Post-Employment Benefit
86: (OPEB) plan that has a special funding situation. The plan is administered through a trust by the
86: Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) Board of Trustees. It is established and administered
86: in accordance with the Texas Insurance Code, Chapter 1575. The Board of Trustees is granted
86: the authority to establish basic and optional group insurance coverage for participants as well as
86: to amend benefit terms as needed under Chapter 1575.052. The Board may adopt rules, plans,
86: procedures, and orders reasonably necessary to administer the program, including minimum
86: benefits and financing standards.
86: OPEB Plan Fiduciary Net Position
86: Detail information about the TRS-Care’s fiduciary net position is available in a separately issued
86: TRS Annual Comprehensive Financial Report that includes financial statements and required
86: supplementary information. That report may be obtained by visiting https://www.trs.texas.gov/
86: learning-resources/publications; by writing to the Teacher Retirement System of Texas at PO Box
86: 149676, Austin, Texas 78714; or by calling 1-800-223-8778.
86: The following information regards to OPEB plan fiduciary net position for the year ending August
86: 31, 2024.
86: Net OPEB Liability Total
86: Total OPEB Liability $ 35,168,178,563
86: Less: Plan Fiduciary Net Position 4,816,646,311
86: Net OPEB Liability $ 30,351,532,252
86: Net Position as % of Total OPEB Liability 13.70%
86: Benefit Provided
86: TRS-Care provides health insurance coverage to retirees from public schools who are members of
86: the TRS pension plan. Optional dependent coverage is available for an additional fee.
86: Eligible non-Medicare retirees and their dependents may enroll in TRS-Care Standard, a high-
86: deductible health plan. Eligible Medicare retirees and their dependents may enroll in the TRS-Care
86: MedicareAdvantage medical plan and the TRS-Care Medicare Rx prescription drug plan. To qualify
86: for TRS-Care coverage, a retiree must have at least 10 years of service credit in the TRS pension
86: system. There are no automatic post-employment benefit changes, including automatic COLAs.

87: Contributions
87: Contribution rates for the TRS-Care plan are established in state statute by the Texas Legislature,
87: and there is no continuing obligation to provide benefits beyond each fiscal year. The TRS-Care
87: plan is currently funded on a pay-as-you-go basis and is subject to change based on available
87: funding. Funding for TRS-Care is provided by retiree premium contributions and contributions from
87: the state, active employees, and school districts based upon public school district payroll. The TRS
87: Board of trustees does not have the authority to set or amend contribution rates.
87: Texas Insurance Code, section 1575.202 establishes the State’s contribution rate which is 1.25
87: percent of the employee’s salary. Section 1575.203 establishes the active employee’s rate which is
87: 0.65 percent of pay. Section 1575.204 establishes an employer’s contribution rate of not less than
87: 0.25percentornotmorethan0.75percentofthesalaryofeachactiveemployeeofthepublicschool.
87: The actual employer contribution rate is prescribed by the Legislature in the GeneralAppropriations
87: Act. The following table shows contributions to the TRS-Care plan by type of contributor.

88: Actuarial Methods and Assumptions
88: The actuarial valuation of the total OPEB liability was performed as of August 31, 2023. Update
88: procedures were used to roll forward the Total OPEB Liability to August 31, 2024.
88: The actuarial valuation of the OPEB plan offered through TRS-Care is similar to the actuarial
88: valuation performed to the pension plan, except that the OPEB valuation is more complex. The
88: demographic assumptions were updated based on the experience study performed for TRS for the
88: period ending August 31, 2021. The following assumptions and other inputs used for members of
88: TRS-Care are based on an established pattern of practice and are identical to the assumptions used
88: in the August 31, 2023 TRS pension actuarial valuation that was rolled forward to August 31, 2024:
88: Rates of Mortality Rates of Disability
88: Rates of Retirement General Inflation
88: Rates of Termination Wage Inflation
88: The active mortality rates were based on PUB (2010), Amount-Weighted, Below-Median Income,
88: Teacher male and female tables (with a two-year set forward for males). The post-retirement
88: mortality rates for healthy lives were based on the 2021 TRS of Texas Healthy Pensioner Mortality
88: Tables. The rates were projected on a fully generational basis using the ultimate improvement rates
88: from the mortality projection scale MP-2021.
88: The following methods and additional assumptions were used in the TRS-Care OPEB valuation:

88: Discount Rate
88: A single discount rate of 3.87 percent was used to measure the total OPEB liability. There was a
88: decrease of 0.26 percent in the discount rate since the previous year. Because the investments are
88: held in cash and there is no intentional objective to advance fund the benefits, the Single Discount
88: Rate is equal to the prevailing municipal bond rate.
88: The source of the municipal bond rate is the Bond Buyer’s “20-Bond GO Index” as of August 31,
88: 2024, using the Fixed Income Municipal bonds with 20 years to maturity that include only federally
88: tax-exempt municipal bonds.

91: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T

91: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
91: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
91: Medicare Part D
91: The Medicare Modernization Act of 2003 (MMA) created an outpatient prescription drug benefit
91: program known as Medicare Part D. The Texas Public School Retired Employee Group Insurance
91: Program (TRS-Care) is offering a Medicare Part D Plan and is participating in the Retiree Drug
91: Subsidy plan for eligible TRS-Care participants. For the years ended August 31, 2025 and August
91: 31, 2024, the subsidy payments received by TRS-Care on behalf of the District were $14,251,299
91: and $7,684,534, respectively. TRS issues a publicly available financial report. That report may be
91: found by visiting the TRS Web site at www.trs.texas.gov/employers/gasb-statements/gasb-24.
91: NOTE 13: COMMITMENTS AND CONTINGENCIES
91: The District received significant financial assistance from numerous federal and state governmental
91: agenciesintheformofgrants.Thedisbursementsoffundsreceivedundertheseprogramsgenerally
91: requires compliance with terms and conditions specified in the grant agreements and are subject
91: to audit by the grantor agencies and the Texas Education Agency. Any disallowed claims resulting
91: from such audits could become a liability of the General Fund.
91: The District is party to various other legal actions, none of which is believed by the administration or
91: legalcounseltohaveamaterialeffectonthefinancialconditionoftheDistrict.Accordingly,noprovision
91: for losses has been recorded in the accompanying financial statements for such contingencies.
91: NOTE 14: NONMONETARY TRANSACTIONS
91: During fiscal year 2025, the District received goods purchased by the State of Texas for the
91: benefit of the District through the Instructional Materials and Technology Allotment program. The
91: instructional materials have been recorded in the amount $1.28 million in the special revenue fund
91: as both state revenues and expenditures, which represent the amount of consideration given by
91: the State of Texas.
91: The District received an award from TEA for the Learning Acceleration Support Opportunities
91: (LASO) Grant which focused on high quality supplemental materials for accelerated instruction
91: activities like high impact tutoring in math for students PK-12 curriculum. In-kind donations in
91: the amount of $667,697 for high quality instructional licenses have been recorded in the special
91: revenue fund as both federal revenues and expenditures in fiscal year 2025.
91: NOTE 15: SUBSEQUENT EVENT
91: Series 2025 Variance Rate Maintenance Tax Notes issued on 7/31/2025 with Par value of
91: $20,555,000.Proceedsofthetax-exempttenderrefundingwereusedtobuybackselectoutstanding
91: bonds tendered by existing Houston ISD investors at the tender price (which is composed of the
91: current market value of those outstanding bonds plus a tender premium) and to pay the costs of
91: issuance. The Maintenance Tax Refunding bonds were structured as serial bonds maturing from
91: 2032 to 2037. The bonds carried a 10-year optional par call, beginning on July 15, 2035, used 5%
91: coupons for all maturities and were structured with uniform savings.

100: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
100: NOTES TO REQUIRED SUPPLEMENTARY INFORMATION
100: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2025
100: Note 1: Stewardship, Compliance, and Accountability
100: The District uses the following procedures in establishing the budgets reflected in the financial
100: statements:
100: • Prior to June 30th of the preceding fiscal year, the District prepares a budget for the next
100: succeedingfiscalyearbeginningJuly1.Theoperatingbudgetincludesproposedexpenditures
100: and the means of financing them.
100: • Public hearings are conducted to obtain citizens’ comments. However, the final priorities
100: and funding of projects are determined by the Board of Managers that establishes a tax rate
100: to support the approved budget.
100: • A meeting of the Board of Managers is then called for the purpose of adopting the proposed
100: budget.
100: • Budgets are approved on a fund-by-fund basis. The District can make amendments to the
100: budget at any time during the year; however, amendments changing functional expenditure
100: categories (as defined in the Texas Education Agency’s Financial Accountability System
100: Resource Guide) must be ratified by the Board of Managers. Budget amendments are
100: presented to the Board of Managers monthly or as needed.
100: • Budgets are considered a management control and planning tool and, as such, are
100: incorporated into the accounting system of the District.
100: • Annual budgets are legally adopted for the General Fund, the Debt Service Fund, and the
100: Nutrition Services Fund. The budgets are prepared on a basis of accounting that is used for
100: reporting in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles and are monitored
100: by the Budget and Financial Planning Office. By state law, expenditures may not legally
100: exceed budgeted appropriations, as amended, at the function level by fund. Unexpended
100: appropriations lapse at the end of the fiscal year.
100: • Per TEA requirements, the expenditure in any functional category should not exceed the
100: approved budget. For the year ended June 30, 2025:
100: General Fund, Function 11, Instruction exceeded budget due to payroll costs and Texas
100: Connections Payment
100: General Fund, Function 21, Instructional Leadership exceeded budget due to payroll costs
100: General Fund, Function 23, School Leadership exceeded budget due to payroll costs
100: General Fund, Function 31, Guidance & Counseling exceeded budget due to payroll costs
100: GeneralFund,Function32,SocialWorkServicesexceededbudgetduetosoftwarepurchases
100: General Fund, Function 33, Health Services exceeded budget due to payroll costs
100: General Fund, Function 52, Security exceeded budget due to overtime costs
100: General Fund, Function 71, Date Processing exceeded budget due to SBITAs
100: General Fund, Function 91, Contracted instructional services between public schools
100: exceeded budget due to an increased local fund assignment which increases the cost
100: of recapture

101: Net Pension Liability
101: Changes in demographic and economic assumptions
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net Pension Liability and Net OPEB Liability:
101: - Demographic assumptions including post-retirement mortality, termination rates, and rates of retirement
101: and economic assumptions, including rates of salary increase for individual participants were updated
101: based on the experience study performed for TRS for the period ending August 31, 2017.
101: Changes in benefit terms
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2024 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - An adjustment of $1.645 billion for September 2023 one-time stipend and $3.355 billion for COLA was
101: made. This appropriation has no impact on the Net Pension Liability.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2023 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - One-time stipend was paid to certain retired teachers in September of 2023 as per legislation passed in
101: 2023. Additionally, cost of living adjustment (COLA) was provided to retirees in January 2024. These
101: payments have no impact on the Net Pension Liability as of August 31,2023.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - Changes of benefit terms were made effective September 1,2017 by the 85th Texas Legislature.
101: Other changes
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2022 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - The participation rate for pre-65 retirees was lowered from 65% to 62%. The participation rate for post-
101: 65 retirees was lowered from 40% to 25%.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2020 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - The participation rate for post-65 retirees was lowered from 50% to 40%.
101: - The ultimate health care trend rate assumption decreased to reflect the repeal of the excise (Cadillac) tax
101: on high-cost employer health plans.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2019 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - With the enactment of SB3 by the 2019 Texas Legislature, as assumption was made about how this
101: would impact future salaries. It is assumed that eligible active members will each receive a $2,700
101: Changes in demographic and economic assumptions
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net Pension Liability and Net OPEB Liability:
101: - Demographic assumptions including post-retirement mortality, termination rates, and rates
101: of retirement and economic assumptions, including rates of salary increase for individual
101: participants were updated based on the experience study performed for TRS for the period
101: ending August 31, 2017.
101: Changes in benefit terms
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2024 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - An adjustment of $1.645 billion for September 2023 one-time stipend and $3.355 billion
101: for COLA was made. This appropriation has no impact on the Net Pension Liability.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2023 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - One-time stipend was paid to certain retired teachers in September of 2023 as per legislation
101: passed in 2023. Additionally, cost of living adjustment (COLA) was provided to retirees
101: in January 2024. These payments have no impact on the Net Pension Liability as of August
101: 31,2023.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - ChangesofbenefittermsweremadeeffectiveSeptember1,2017bythe85thTexasLegislature.
101: Other changes
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2022 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - The participation rate for pre-65 retirees was lowered from 65% to 62%. The participation
101: rate for post-65 retirees was lowered from 40% to 25%.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2020 – Net OPEB Liability:
101: - The participation rate for post-65 retirees was lowered from 50% to 40%.
101: - The ultimate health care trend rate assumption decreased to reflect the repeal of the excise
101: (Cadillac) tax on high-cost employer health plans.
101: For Measurement Date August 31, 2019 – Net Pension Liability:
101: - With the enactment of SB3 by the 2019 Texas Legislature, as assumption was made about
101: how this would impact future salaries. It is assumed that eligible active members will each
101: receive a $2,700 increase in fiscal year 2020. This is in addition to the salary increase
101: expected based on the actuarial assumptions.
102: - 90 -
102: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
102: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
102: For Measurement Date August 31, 2019 – Net OPEB Liability:
102: - The participation rate for pre-65 retirees was lowered from 70% to 65%. The participation
102: rate for post-65 retirees was lowered from 75% to 50%. 25% of pre-65 retirees are assumed
102: to discontinue their coverage at age 65.
102: - The trend rates were reset to better reflect the plan’s anticipated experience.
102: - The percentage of retirees who are assumed to have two-person coverage was lowered
102: from 20% to 15%. In addition, the participation assumption for the surviving spouses
102: of employees that die while actively employed was lowered from 20% to 10%.
102: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net OPEB Liability:
102: - Adjustments were made for retirees that were known to have discontinued their health care
102: coverage in fiscal year 2018.
102: - The health care trend rate assumption was updated to reflect the anticipated return of the
102: Health Insurer Fee (HIF) in 2020.

105: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
105: Nonmajor Enterprise Funds
105: NONMAJOR ENTERPRISE FUNDS
105: NONMAJOR ENTERPRISE FUNDS
105: The Medicaid Fund is used to account for the financial operations of the district’s school-based federal Medicaid
105: program that provides health and medical services to Medicaid eligible special education students. In addition,
105: the district currently provides consulting services and Medicaid claims management services to approximately 90
105: Texas school districts.
105: The Marketplace Fund is used to account for the financial operations of the Starbucks® coffee and snack bar
105: located on the first level of the Hattie Mae White (HMW) Educational Support Center. The district established this
105: Marketplace as a benefit to employees working in the HMW building. The Starbucks® coffee and snack bar
105: provides a variety Starbucks® drinks and grab-and-go items made available to guests and employees at HMW.

109: - 95 -
109: H O U S T O N I N D E P E N D E N T
109: S C H O O L D I S T R I C T
109: Internal Service Funds
109: INTERNAL SERVICE FUNDS
109: INTERNAL SERVICE FUNDS
109: The Print Shop Fund is used to account for all the printing and distribution activities of the District’s print shop.
109: Schools and departments are charged for the costs of printing and distribution requests. During non-peak
109: times, the Print Shop contracts with other governmental agencies requiring assistance during their peak
109: periods. In addition, the Print Shop handles the districtwide copier contract/services for all schools and
109: departments.
109: The Health Insurance Fund is used to account for the health insurance plan administered by the District. The
109: plan is a self-insured plan requiring the District and its employees to contribute the cost. The District
109: contributes approximately 81.45 percent of the annual cost of the plan with employees paying for the remaining
109: 18.55 percent. These contributions are paid from all governmental and proprietary funds to the Health
109: Insurance Fund from which all claims and administrative expenses are paid.
109: The Workers’ Compensation Fund is used to account for risk financing activities related to the self-insured
109: workers’ compensation program. All employees of the District are covered by this plan for injuries occurring on
109: the job. The District contributes 100 percent of the funding for this program.
109: The Internal Services Fund is used to account for the fee-for-services offered by the District to serve the
109: specific needs of schools and departments. The Alternative Certification, Athletics, UIL, and Shared Services
109: programs are accounted for within this fund.

121: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
121: STATISTICAL INFORMATION
121: (Unaudited)
121: The Statistical Section of the District’s annual comprehensive financial report presents detailed information as a
121: context for understanding what the information in the financial statements, note disclosures, and required
121: supplementary information says about the District’s overall financial health.
121: CONTENTS
121: Financial Trends
121: These schedules contain information to help the reader understand how the District’s financial performance has
121: changed over time.
121: Revenue Capacity
121: These schedules contain information to help the reader assess the District’s major revenue sources.
121: Debt Capacity
121: These schedules contain information to help the reader assess the affordability of the District’s current levels of
121: outstanding debt and the District’s ability to issue additional debt in the future.
121: Demographic and Economic Information
121: These schedules offer demographic and economic indicators to help the reader understand the environment
121: within which the District’s financial activities take place.
121: Operating Information
121: These schedules contain service and infrastructure data to help the reader understand how the information in
121: the district’s financial report relates to the services the District provides and activities it performs.
121: SOURCES
121: Unless otherwise noted, the information in these schedules is derived from the annual comprehensive financial
121: reports for the relevant year.

130: NOTES: Governmental Funds expenditures include those made by the following funds: General, Special Revenue, Debt Service, Capital Project, and the nonmajor governmental fund (Public Facility Corporation) for 2016-2022
130: (1) The payment to escrow agents - current refundings in fiscal years 2016, 2017, 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022, and 2023 have been deducted from non-capital expenditures in order to insure comparability between
130: years, as these amounts are remarketing of variable interest debt, not a true debt refunding.
130: (2) Debt service ratio is equal to (Principal+Interest+payments to escrow agents)/ (Total Expenditures-Total Capital Assets Being Depreciated - as reported in the Reconciliation of the Governmental Funds Statement
130: of Revenues, Expenditures, and Changes in Fund Balances to the Statement of Activities.
130: The 2025 Debt Service Ratio is calculated as follows:
130: (297,777,665 + 92,515,941) / (3,150,880,654 - 62,245,990) where 62,245,990 is total Capital Assets Being Depreciated as reflected in the Reconciliation of the Governmental Funds Statement of
130: Revenues, Expenditures, and Changes in Fund Balances to the Statement of Activities.
130: The ratio of 18.47% was restated from the original 19.59% published in fiscal year 2019.

141: Local Excess Revenue (Recapture)
141: Local
141: Excess
141: Fiscal Year Revenue
141: 2017 $ 91,544,985
141: 2018 $ 168,325,648
141: 2019 $ 185,111,161
141: 2020 $ 74,910,822 *
141: 2021 $ 198,143,888
141: 2022 $ 186,526,718
141: 2023 $ 275,327,222
141: 2024 $ - **
141: 2025 $ 56,900,029
141: In fiscal year 2017, Houston ISD began sending "local excess revenue" to the state.
141: The district is considered property rich and is required to send back local tax revenue
141: in excess of entitlement.
141: Chapter 49 of the Texas Education Code (TEC) makes provisions for certain school districts to
141: share their local tax revenue with other school districts. These provisions are sometimes referred
141: to as "share the wealth", "Robin Hood" or "recapture" because recaptured funds are redistributed
141: by the school finance system.
141: * Decrease in local excess revenue payment due to impact of HB3 which revised the formula
141: ** No payment required due to tax compression and $100k homestead exemption
141: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
141: LOCAL EXCESS REVENUE (RECAPTURE)
141: FOR THE LAST NINE FISCAL YEARS ENDING JUNE 30 HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
141: LOCAL EXCESS REVENUE (RECAPTURE)
141: FOR THE LAST NINE FISCAL YEARS ENDING JUNE 30
141: In fiscal year 2017, Houston ISD began sending “local excess revenue” to the state. The district is
141: considered property rich and is required to send back local tax revenue in excess of entitlement.
141: Chapter 49 of the Texas Education Code (TEC) makes provisions for certain school districts to
141: share their local tax revenue with other school districts. These provisions are sometimes referred
141: to as “share the wealth”, “Robin Hood” or “recapture” because recaptured funds are redistributed
141: by the school finance system.
141: * Decrease in local excess revenue payment due to impact of HB3 which revised the formula
141: ** No payment required due to tax compression and $100k homestead exemption
